# Design: the issue-polling orchestrator (`/sc:orchestrator`)

Status: draft for owner review, written 2026-11-19 for IronbellyOrg/IronClaude issue 655 (`/sc:design --type architecture --format spec`). It holds no implementation code. Nothing that depends on it starts before the owner (Clay) approves it.

Grounding: graph:unlisted (the codegraph MCP tools were not available for the whole run; every step used Glob, Grep and Read, and every `file:line` below was read from the file).

Evidence: each statement about today's code or tools cites a `file:line` in this worktree (base `origin/master` 89dc403d4), a command I ran, or a file on disk. The one-line evidence list is `.dev/orchestrator-655/spec/evidence.md` (ids E1 to E165, not committed). A statement I could not verify is marked UNVERIFIED and section 12 says how to verify it.

Inputs read in full: issue 655 body and its comments, the history comment, the coordinator spec `SPEC-AGENT-WORK-COORDINATOR-20261027.md` (FR-1 to FR-28), issue 651, the accuracy check of 655, and Orca's `orchestration` skill (`orca skills get orchestration`).

Writing rules: plain English; no em dashes; "FR-n" means a requirement of the coordinator spec; "wave n" means a wave of the orchestrator skill (issue section 2); "the user" means the person the orchestrator works for (Clay).

## 1. Scope, goals and the fixed rulings

### 1.1 What this design is

One system replaces the built `superclaude issue-watch` poller, the plan-approval gate that follows its claim, and the separate watcher session (issue 655, replacing #450 and the open parts of #331):

```
Orca automation (every 5 min, precheck only, no agent)
  -> poll script (`superclaude issue-watch once`, IronClaude Python)
       reads: GitHub (assigned issues + 3 information streams), repo list from the orchestrator repo
       routes each new issue to an orchestrator of its repo with a free place, or starts one (5.7)
       writes: one wake file + mailbox message per orchestrator, then types ONE fixed line into that session
               and, for an issue that fails the plan check, one label on GitHub (and no comment, ruling 33) (4.11);
               for an issue held an hour with no plan, one plan request in the wake
  -> orchestrator session (one per repo, at most 3 issues in flight each, in the orchestrator repo; `/sc:orchestrator`)
       wave 0 intake -> 1 claim -> 2 plan check -> 3 report entry -> 4 handoff + worker-start
       -> 5 watch -> 6 PR watch -> 7 merge -> 8 close -> 9 completion
  -> workers (Orca `worker-start`, in the issue's own repo and worktree)
```

The orchestrator keeps the work report current as a living document all day: an entry is added at claim, updated at milestones and removed at close. The daily run no longer touches issue entries; it adds only what the orchestrator does not cover (ClickUp tasks and messages, direct messages, mentions, items with no issue and the like), and the daily report itself is being phased out in favour of the living report (owner ruling 1, 2026-11-19; DQ12). There is no session-manager skill, now or later: the orchestrator does every check-in itself, including typing into a worker's terminal and approving its permission prompts (owner ruling 2, DQ9). Only the user's own GitHub login counts as the user: there is no bot list and no field for one (owner ruling 11, DQ1).

With nothing new, no agent starts and no model tokens are spent: the poll is a script and the automation never starts a session (the precheck exits non-zero, the pattern of `waker.py`, evidence E130).

### 1.2 Goals

- G1. An issue assigned to the user (the user's own GitHub login; there is no bot list) in any listed repo, in either GitHub org, is claimed once and its worker started once, with no human step, within one poll interval plus the claim time, for an issue that passes the plan check (an incomplete issue waits for the check, and a plan is created through `/sc:issue` only after one hour with no change and no sign of plan work elsewhere, 4.11) (plus the rest of the turn the orchestrator session is in, if it is claiming another issue at that moment; the 07:00 report does not delay it because the report runs in a different session, DQ12). A repo with more than 3 issues in flight gets another orchestrator, so a fourth issue does not wait behind the first three (G5).
- G2. The orchestrator makes sure the issue has a plan (Priority, Fix approach, Test plan, the format of #651), starts a supervised worker in the issue's own repo and worktree, watches the worker and its pull request, merges when every condition holds, closes the issue and keeps the work report current.
- G3. Every safety feature of the built watcher that issue 655 lists as carried over still holds and still has a test (section 7.1).
- G4. Everything this system keeps while it runs lives in the orchestrator repo; every skill lives in IronClaude (issue 655, "Where things live").
- G5. One orchestrator per repo, at most 3 issues in flight per orchestrator (`max_in_flight` in `repos.yaml`); the poll routes each new issue to an orchestrator of its repo with a free place or starts one, and no two orchestrators claim the same issue (5.7). A worker pushes its own branch and opens its pull request, never merges and never force-pushes; only the orchestrator merges (owner ruling 3).
- G6. Stronger models plan and cheaper models build: the plan-writing agent, the building worker and the orchestrator session each run on a ccsession tier named in `repos.yaml` (`plan_tier`, `worker_tier`, `orchestrator_tier`; a tier name, never a model id; Clay, 2026-11-19 03:09 UTC; 5.8).

### 1.3 Non-goals (issue 655, "Out of Scope")

- Creating issues and the Priority, Fix approach and Test plan sections themselves (#651).
- The general orchestrator for all sessions of #339. #444 proposes a `/sc:orchestrator` command with a `--sub` mode; this design gives the name `/sc:orchestrator` to the issue-polling orchestrator (decision OQ5) and leaves `--sub` to #444 (#444 is an open evaluation issue, `gh issue view 444`, evidence E140; how it joins is UNVERIFIED).
- A separate merge agent, a listener, a webhook, a GitHub Action, a hosted service.
- A separate session-manager skill: there is none, now or later (decision DQ9).

### 1.4 The fixed rulings (issue 655 section 10 and its comments; not reopened here)

| # | Ruling (Clay, 2026-11-18) | Where this design applies it |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Assignment is the only trigger: no trigger label, no approval label, no approver list, no plan-approval gate after the claim. The hold of 4.11 is a precondition, not a trigger: assignment is still the only trigger, no person has to set or approve anything for work to start (a person may stop an issue with `hold:backlog`), and the check is a script | sections 4.2, 7.2 |
| R2 | Assignment is safe as the only trigger because only users with triage or write access can assign (stated by the owner; this design checks it against GitHub's documents: decision OQ6) | section 2.2 |
| R3 | The Backlog column may stay on the board but nothing may depend on it | sections 4.2, 7.2 |
| R4 | An Orca automation runs the schedule, not cron | section 4.8 |
| R5 | A launch outcome that is unknown is checked by the orchestrator itself and retried once; no command is printed for a person | sections 5.3 (wave 4), 7.1 |
| R6 | GitHub throttling uses exponential backoff; an unrecognized GitHub error is treated as a login failure until the live test shows the real text | sections 4.6, 7.1 |
| R7 | The poll sends data to an agent only when something new arrived since the last poll | sections 3, 4.3 |
| R8 | The live test uses throwaway issues that are only assigned (no labels, no column); the poll writes `hold:plan` on one that is incomplete (T11) | section 9 |
| R9 | The orchestrator claims at `--pipeline none` and starts the worker itself with `orca orchestration worker-start`; the worker reports through the Run mailbox; the claim's own launch levels are never used | section 5.3 (waves 1 and 4) |
| R10 | The orchestrator, not the worker, merges and closes; the worker never merges; closing an issue and merging are off the human-only list | sections 5.3 (waves 7 and 8), 5.6 |
| R11 | The skill is `orchestrator`, the command `/sc:orchestrator`, built in IronClaude as a thin command plus a protocol skill folder; the skill is ONE pull request, tested end to end locally before its pull request is opened | sections 5, 8 |
| R12 | The four templates are real files, each with a test; every step that writes or sends something fills its template | section 5.4 |
| R13 | The eight escalation rules, in the numbered form of issue 655 section 9 | section 5.6 |
| R14 | All skills live in IronClaude; the coordinator spec, the work report state, the daily run's records, the repo list, the repo-to-checkout map and the repo overviews live in the orchestrator repo; the IronFlow copy of the spec is kept in sync, not the home | section 6 |
| R15 | The repo list and the repo-to-checkout map are never kept in IronClaude configuration | sections 4.4, 6.2 |

### 1.5 Terms

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Orchestrator session | A Claude Code session with ccsession tag `orchestrator-<short>-<n>` (decisions DQ12 and 5.7), running in a terminal of the orchestrator repo's Orca workspace, that runs `/sc:orchestrator` for one repo and holds at most 3 issues in flight; there is one per repo, and another is started when the repo has more. The report session, tag `coordinator`, is a separate single session in the same workspace |
| Orchestrator id | `<short>-<n>`: the repo's short name (5.7) and a number; it names the session tag `orchestrator-<id>`, the Orca Run, the file `.orchestrator/runs/<id>.json` and the `orchestrator` field of each state file |
| Orchestrator repo | The private repo `IronbellyOrg/IronOrchestrator` (OD8; this design writes `<orchestrator-repo>`), where the orchestrator sessions run |
| Poll script | `superclaude issue-watch once`, the reshaped built watcher |
| Wake | One delivery of the poll's findings to the orchestrator session (section 3) |
| Run | An Orca orchestration Run (`orca orchestration run-create`), one for the orchestrator, resumed with `run-use` |
| Worker | A supervised agent started by `orca orchestration worker-start` |
| Claim checkout | The checkout of an issue's own repo named in the repo-to-checkout map, in which `/sc:issue --claim` runs (decision ADD2) |


## 2. Decisions

Each row has the decision, the reason, the evidence and a flag: "decided (ODn)" means the choice changes what the product does and the owner's rulings settled it (section 10); "no" is settled here and the owner can still change it when approving this design.

### 2.1 The 13 design questions of issue 655 section 14

| ID | Question | Decision | Reason | Evidence | Owner |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| DQ1 | Which login counts as "the user" | Only the user's own GitHub login: the login that `gh api user` returns, checked every cycle against `owner_account_id`. There is no bot list and no field for one (owner ruling 11, 2026-11-19). An issue qualifies when it is an open issue (not a pull request), the user is an assignee, there is no other assignee and the issue passes the hold check of 4.11. Any other assignee (a co-assignee) gives the skip reason `assigned_other` | Keeps the built rule that an issue another person holds is never taken (today the set must be exactly the own login) and adds nothing |  `predicates.py:162-174` (`is_intended_owner`); `cycle.py:752-769` (login and `owner_account_id` check); issue 655 section 14 test row 2 | decided (ruling 11) |
| DQ2 | How the wake finds the orchestrator session; down or busy | Each wake is addressed to exactly one orchestrator (one per repo, several when a repo has more than 3 issues in flight; the routing is section 5.7). The poll finds that orchestrator's session as the terminal in the orchestrator repo's Orca workspace whose running Claude process has its ccsession tag `orchestrator-<short>-<n>`, looked up every time, never by title. It delivers on two channels: (a) durable: the wake file plus an Orca mailbox message to `run:<run id>` (the Run id of that orchestrator, from `.orchestrator/runs/<id>.json`); (b) only when the session is idle at its prompt, has no unsent typing and no output in the last 60 seconds: one typed line `/sc:orchestrator --wake <wake_id>`. Busy or typing: no line is typed, channel (a) alone carries the wake and a session blocked in `check --wait` reads it at once. No acknowledgment in 10 minutes: the same issues go out again in a new wake (attempt 2, then 3); after attempt 3 the entry is `abandoned` with reason `ack_not_observed`, the cycle exits 7 and the poll marks the orchestrator workspace unread with a note (`orca worktree set --worktree path:<orchestrator repo> --unread --comment <note>`). Session down (no live session of that tag) or no orchestrator of the repo at all: the poll starts one, `ccsession orchestrator-<short>-<n> --tier <orchestrator_tier>` (5.8) in a terminal of that workspace, as `waker.py` does. This is the design (owner ruling 4, 2026-11-19), not a question; only when the start itself fails does it record `no_session` and mark the workspace unread | A typed line cannot reach a session that is busy for hours (wave 5 keeps the session in a `check --wait` loop while any worker runs), so the durable channel is needed; the typed line is what wakes an idle session. A wake file plus an acknowledgment gives at-least-once delivery, and claim idempotence (DQ3) makes a repeat harmless | `orca_term.py:31-58` (find by process, never title), `:91-99` (typing or fresh output), `:102-107` (send with receipt); `waker.py:214-229`, `:144-169` (start); `waker.py:239-258` (a turn start alone does not prove the wake); `orchestration send --help`: "a sender in no Run must use run:<id>" (E114) | decided (OD2) |
| DQ3 | What stops a double claim between two orchestrators or two users, and who wins | Four layers, in order. (1) The poll is the only router: it gives each issue to exactly one orchestrator of its repo under its lock, writes the dispatch record (with that orchestrator's id) before any wake, never re-routes it, and allows at most 3 wake attempts per issue; an orchestrator refuses a wake that is not addressed to it (5.7). (2) The orchestrator creates the per-issue state file with exclusive create (`O_EXCL`) before wave 1, so one host never runs two intakes for one issue. (3) The claim itself stops for a closed issue, a pull request, or an issue assigned to someone else. (4) The claim does NOT stop a second orchestrator running as the same login ("already assigned to the current login: continue, re-claim is allowed and idempotent"). Layers 1 and 2 keep two orchestrators of one host apart; this layer is the second line that arbitrates what the poll cannot see (two hosts that each run a poll, two users' orchestrators, a hand-run `--take`, a store restored from a backup). So before wave 1 the orchestrator posts a lease comment `<!-- sc:orchestrator:lease host=<host> run=<run id> -->`, lists the comments with that marker written by its own login, and keeps the claim only when its own lease has the lowest comment id; the loser edits its lease to `released`, records `claimed_elsewhere` and takes nothing. Two users: only the assigned user takes the issue; when both users are assignees each sees the other as a foreign assignee (DQ1) and neither takes it, and each gets an information entry `co_assigned` | The assignee check alone fails for two orchestrators of one user, which is the case the issue's local test names. A lowest-comment-id lease needs no new GitHub feature and no clock agreement between hosts | `refs/claim.md:36-43` (Wave 2: closed, assigned elsewhere stop; "re-claim is allowed and idempotent"); `refs/claim.md:30` and `claim-comment.md` (the marker has no host or session id); `record.py:538-568` (record before the act) | no |
| DQ4 | A repo with no local checkout: set it up or escalate | The repo must be in `repos.yaml`. Listed, enabled, with its `checkout` missing on disk and a `clone_parent` set: wave 1 runs `orca project setup-clone --project github:<owner>/<repo> --host local --url https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>.git --destination <clone_parent>` once, re-reads the path, runs the origin check and writes the checkout into `repos.yaml`. Not listed, disabled, clone failed or origin check failed: no claim, no worker; an unlisted or disabled repo is an information entry, a failed clone or origin check is a failed handoff (escalation rule 8). The worker never starts in a checkout whose `origin` is not the issue's repo | The coordinator spec already decided to set up a missing workspace (FR-8, Q12: "since approvals are broad"); the origin check is the built control that keeps a worker out of the wrong repo (`check_origin`) | FR-8 and Q12 in the coordinator spec; `orca project setup-clone --help` (E117); `launch.py:233-252` | no |
| DQ5 | What state survives a restart and where it is kept | Five stores, each the authority for one thing. Orca: the Run, Tasks, Dispatches, gates and references (resumed with `run-use --id`). `<orchestrator-repo>/.orchestrator/runs/<id>.json`: one file per orchestrator with its Run id, host, creation time and status (5.7). `<orchestrator-repo>/.orchestrator/issues/<owner>__<repo>__<N>.json`: one file per issue with the orchestrator that holds it, the wave reached and a write-ahead step log (section 5.5). The poll's `dispatch.json` in `state_dir`: wakes sent and acknowledged, each entry with the orchestrator it was routed to; and `.orchestrator/orchestrators.json`, the registry of orchestrators, written only by the poll. GitHub: the assignee, the claim marker, the lease comment, the plan marker. `.orchestrator/` is git-ignored: it holds host paths and Run ids that mean nothing on another machine. A restart resumes every file whose status is `in_progress` at its recorded step and never repeats a step whose result it cannot read back | The coordinator spec rejects database-level crash recovery and asks only for no second start (FR-8, FR-2 note); write-ahead plus a read-back of the outside state gives that | FR-8 text; `record.py:34-36`, `:538-568` (durable pending write before the act); `SKILL.md:140` of `sc-issue-protocol` (an unfinished worktree step is settled by reading `worker-list`) | no |
| DQ6 | The missing-plan agent: how it starts and the grant that lets it write | The plan call (4.11 point 5, wave 0) runs `/sc:issue --plan <N>`; the skill's own workers draft the three sections and the calling session writes them. The grant is a standing grant, the `grants` list of `repos.yaml`, with the entries `issue.assign`, `issue.comment`, `issue.body.append_plan_sections`, `issue.hold` (which the poll reads and uses for its two label writes, 4.4), `pr.merge` and `issue.close`; each is allowed only on a listed repo, for an issue assigned to the user and routed to this orchestrator (a claim, or a plan request), and the body write covers only the three plan sections and the marker of the skill (6.2). Anything not listed is refused | Workers never write to GitHub (WC-9; `sc-issue-protocol` writes only from the orchestrator, `SKILL.md:74`), so the grant belongs to the orchestrator. A standing grant is needed because there is no person at each step: the old per-issue "grant row" came from the approval flow that this issue retires | `WORKER-CONTRACT.md:44` (WC-9), `:300` (issue write needs a grant row, retires with the duties); `SKILL.md:74`; issue 655 "Architecture Impact" | no |
| DQ7 | Whether a `worker-start` worker at `--pipeline none` falls under the #442 gate | No. The launch-level guard governs only a session bound by the strict launch marker `[sc-launch:v1:<32 hex>]` plus a pending nonce; an unbound session with no marker is allowed. A `worker-start` worker has no marker, so the guard never sees it and the #442 observations (OB-H1 items 1 to 9, OB-H2, OB-H3, OB-CTX) do not gate it. The ship gate in `refs/claim.md:136` and `SKILL.md:92` is written for the claim's own `worktree` and `full` launches, which the orchestrator never uses. The price: these workers run with no L1 guard and no L2 pre-push hook (`install_l2` runs only in the claim's launch step). What bounds them instead is the handoff prompt, WORKER-CONTRACT (decision ADD1) and the orchestrator's merge and escalation rules: the worker pushes its own feature branch and opens its pull request, never merges and never force pushes, and only the orchestrator merges (owner ruling 3, 2026-11-19). This is a risk and not a question: `master` of IronClaude is not protected (`protected: false`, DQ8), so nothing on GitHub stops a worker that ignores its prompt from pushing to it; K12 records it. The owner already accepted the same condition for the first test issue of the coordinator handoff ("G1"). A test fails if the skill launches through `orca_worktree.py launch` | The guard's own design and header say so; the claim text scopes the gate to governed claims | `sc-issue-launch-level-hook.md:77` ("Unbound with no marker: allow"); `launch-level-guard.sh:1-8` (only a claim-launched session is governed, FR-L-53); `issue-pipeline-guard-g2.md:5-8` and `issue-pipeline-fu1.md` (G1: coordinator-started worker runs without the guard); `refs/claim.md:136`; `SKILL.md:92`; `gh issue view 442` (12 observation tests skipped, issue open) | decided (OD1) |
| DQ8 | Merge method and branch protection of each repo; no merge rights | `repos.yaml` holds, per repo: `merge_method` for an ordinary pull request and `release_merge_method` for a release pull request, one whose head is the repo's `release_head` branch and whose base is its default branch (each `squash`, `merge` or `rebase`; the repo's normal practice, no question asked, owner ruling 5, 2026-11-19: IronClaude `squash` for ordinary pull requests, whose history shows `(#NNN)` squash titles, and `merge` for a release pull request from `integration` to `master`, because the squash merge of release 6.1.0 (#658) was reverted by #662; IronFlow and IronOrca `merge`, read from the merge commits with two parents of all 2 and all 3 of their merged pull requests, a sample too small to call a settled practice, UNVERIFIED), `protected` (read when the repo is added, with the default branch), `can_merge` (from `permissions` of `gh api repos/<R>`: push, maintain or admin) and `reviewers` (decision ADD4). The merge command is `gh pr merge <N> --repo <R> --<method> --match-head-commit <final head sha>` and never carries `--auto`, `--admin` or `--delete-branch` (decision ADD5). The user lacks rights, `can_merge` is false, `mergeStateStatus` is not clean, or `gh pr merge` refuses (protection, required review, merge queue): no retry, no bypass; the entry goes to state `gated`, an Orca gate is created and the case is escalated as row 9 of 5.6 (an action only a person with those rights can take is a "stuck" case, not a decision). IronClaude today: all three methods allowed, auto-merge off, branch deletion on merge off, `master` not protected (`protected: false`, no rulesets), and the user holds `maintain`. Across the two orgs the user is admin on 11 of 48 IronbellyOrg repos and on all 17 IronbellyGroup repos, maintain on 2, and read-only on 35, so `repos.yaml` lists only repos where `can_merge` is true as merge-enabled | The merge method is a per-repo setting GitHub already enforces; recording it avoids guessing. Pinning the head SHA makes "merge only the head every check ran on" a GitHub-side guarantee. The classic protection detail is readable only with admin rights (the API answers 404 for `maintain`), so it cannot be a planning input | `gh api repos/IronbellyOrg/IronClaude` (merge settings and `permissions`, E150); `gh api repos/IronbellyOrg/IronClaude/branches/master` (`protected: false`); `rules/branches/master` and `rulesets` both `[]`; `branches/master/protection` 404 for a non-admin; `.dev/orchestrator-655/spec/org-repos-*.tsv`; `gh pr merge --help` (`--match-head-commit`, `--admin`, `--auto`, `--delete-branch`); GitHub "About merge methods" (all three need write access; a rule can require one method; a merge queue picks the method) | decided (OD3) |
| DQ9 | The session-manager skill: separate or part of the orchestrator | Part of the orchestrator skill (owner ruling, 2026-11-18 21:20 UTC). There is no separate session-manager skill, now or later, and no `agent-session-manager` folder is built. The orchestrator skill itself does the check-ins, types into worker terminals and approves a worker's permission prompts, under the playbook (FR-23) and the approval rules (FR-24). Wave 5 and `refs/playbook.md` cover both: a check-in goes by the Orca mailbox first (`send --to dispatch:<id>`, `ask`, `reply`), and a typed key or an approval goes by `orca terminal send` into the worker's terminal under the safety rules of wave 5 (5.3). The typed parts of FR-10, FR-23 and FR-24 are in scope of the orchestrator pull request (PR B, which grows by the typing and approval code path, its playbook rows and their tests, 8.1); FR-10's subagent wording changes in the coordinator spec (6.3, C21) | The owner ruled it. One skill that owns the worker from handoff to merge has no second skill to start, no handoff package and no handback to check. Typing into a terminal is the riskiest step, so the FR-24 safety rules are tests and not only prose (9.1) | FR-1, FR-10, FR-23, FR-24 (coordinator spec); `orca terminal send --help` (a prompt to a recognized agent is bracketed-pasted; `--wait-submit` observes submission and never resends; `--retry-request` repeats the exact command); `orca_term.py:75-99` (`terminal read --screen` gives `tail` and `draft`; typing or fresh output is the hold); `WORKER-CONTRACT.md` WC-12 | no |
| DQ10 | Poll cadence against the waker (FR-25) and the hours of the worker check | The poll is its own Orca automation `orchestrator-poll`, trigger `*/5 * * * *`, 24 hours a day and 7 days a week (an idle poll is a script that costs no tokens, and an assignment at night is claimed and worked). It is independent of the waker automations: the waker keeps the Monday to Friday daily run (default 07:00 local) and the 09:00 missed-run check, and the worker status check has no automation of its own: while a worker runs, wave 5 keeps each orchestrator session in a `check --wait` loop and does the check for that worker itself once an hour by default (the interval is configurable, as FR-2 of the coordinator spec says; its key is not designed here), on Monday to Friday, 24 hours a day (owner ruling 7, 2026-11-19; a typed hourly line could not land on a session that is never idle). The check for a worker stops the moment that worker sends `worker_done`, or wave 9 settles it; with no worker in flight there is nothing to check; `/sc:orchestrator --hourly` runs the same check once on request. There is no working-hours setting: the loop reads the host's local weekday itself, so Orca's lack of time-zone support (`waker.py:37-39`) does not matter. The report and the orchestrators are separate sessions (DQ12, 5.7), so a report run never delays a poll wake and a worker wait loop never blocks a report or missed-run wake; a poll wake that finds its orchestrator busy travels on the mailbox (DQ2) | FR-25 describes a waker for Monday to Friday fixed times; the issue needs assignments picked up within five minutes at any time. Two sessions with two jobs share only the orchestrator repo's files; a typed wake lands only on an idle session with no output in the last 60 seconds, and the morning wake gives up after 15 tries 120 seconds apart and the hourly wake after one, so a session that is never idle cannot take them | `waker.py:184`, `:199-201`, `:225-229` and `orca_term.py:91-99` (the idle and fresh-output rules); `waker.py:27-29` (hourly wake line), `:34-52` (`local_gate`, Orca ignores the time zone, verified 2026-10-30); `agent-work-report/config.json` (`daily_run_local`, `timezone`, `report_days`); `orca automations list` (only `coordinator-morning-wake` and `coordinator-missed-check` exist today); `automations create --help` (E113) | decided (OD5) |
| DQ11 | The format of the wake message | Section 3: a JSON wake file (`orchestrator-wake/1`) holding every fact, plus a fixed typed line and a mailbox message that carry only the wake id | One format both PR A and PR B build against; no GitHub text reaches a typed line | section 3 | no |
| DQ12 | How the work report is built and served once its state is in the orchestrator repo; how the `coordinator-` automations move | Separate sessions in the orchestrator repo's Orca workspace: the single report session (tag `coordinator`, unchanged) runs `/agent-work-report`, and the orchestrators (one per repo, several when a repo has more than 3 issues in flight, tag `orchestrator-<short>-<n>`, section 5.7) run `/sc:orchestrator`. FR-2 put both jobs in one session, but wave 5 keeps an orchestrator in a `check --wait` loop while any of its workers runs, so it is never idle, the report and missed-run wakers (typed lines, sent only to an idle prompt with no output in the last 60 seconds, `waker.py:225-229`, `orca_term.py:91-99`) could not land on it, and a report run of up to 120 minutes would delay every new assignment (OD6). PR C moves the whole `agent-work-report` folder (SKILL.md 183 lines, `config.json`, 15 scripts, 7 templates) to `src/superclaude/skills/agent-work-report/`. Two code changes: `common.py` `repo_root()` (today the git root of the skill's own folder, `common.py:19-26`, which fails once the skill runs from `~/.claude/skills/`) returns `$AWR_ROOT` or the orchestrator repo path; `config.json` loses its `workspace` key (today `/home/coder/workspaces/worktrees/IronFlow/coordinator`) and reads the repo path from `repos.yaml`. State stays at `.dev/coordinator/` but inside the orchestrator repo. The orchestrator keeps the work report current as a living document all day (owner ruling 1, 2026-11-19): it adds an entry when it claims an issue, updates it at milestones and removes it at close. It writes one entry file per orchestrated issue at `.dev/coordinator/orchestrated/<owner>__<repo>__<N>.json` using the slot names of `item_written` (`common.py:244-255`); the entry file's slot set is stated in wave 3 (5.3). The daily run no longer touches issue entries at all: it covers only what the orchestrator does not (ClickUp tasks and messages, direct messages, mentions the orchestrator's streams did not record, items with no issue, sessions under no orchestrator, and a missed-update sweep), and the daily report itself is being phased out in favour of the living report. The daily run is changed in four places (PR C): (1) a new step `exclude.py`, run between Step 4 (agent sessions) and Step 5 (`match.py`), removes from the run's `github-items.json` every issue that has an entry file in `orchestrated/` (the orchestrator holds it), and only while this run's collection shows that issue open and assigned to the user in a repo it read without error (`coverage.json`); it also removes the pull request the entry names and, from `sessions.json`, the worker and orchestrator sessions it names, and writes the removed urls to `excluded.json`, so `to_write` (`common.py:211-217`), `render.py`, `check.py` and `page.py` never see them. An issue that has an entry file but that the run does not show open and assigned (a person closed it or took the assignment away) is not excluded; `run.py refresh` reconciles it (below). Entry files in `orchestrated/closed/` and `orchestrated/info/` exclude nothing, so a reopened issue and an information item are listed by the daily run like any other item. (2) `compare.py` (`main`, lines 73-122) drops the urls of `excluded.json` from the previous snapshot it compares against, so an excluded item is neither new, changed nor closed. (3) `check.py` (carry-forward check, lines 127-133) skips the urls of `excluded.json`, so an item that left the daily run for the living report is not reported as lost. (4) SKILL.md Step 12 ends with `run.py refresh --run <run>` (S1 below). ClickUp tasks stay with the daily run: `match.py` gives a ClickUp task that matched no GitHub item its own item (`match.py` docstring, lines 2 to 9), so a task linked to an orchestrated issue becomes an item of its own once the issue is removed from `github-items.json`, and nothing about it is written by the orchestrator, whose entry shows `N/A` for ClickUp tasks (UNVERIFIED by a run, U27). The living report is built by a new script verb `run.py refresh` (PR C), with no model call: (a) it reconciles each entry file in `orchestrated/` with `gh issue view <N> --repo <R> --json state,stateReason,assignees`: a closed issue, or one the user is no longer assigned to, moves its entry to `orchestrated/closed/` with GitHub's real reason (`stateReason`, such as `COMPLETED` or `NOT_PLANNED`, or "no longer assigned to you"); (b) it deletes files in `orchestrated/closed/` and `orchestrated/info/` older than `info_ttl_days` (14, one key for both); (c) it takes the `overview.md` blocks of the last completed daily run for the items that are not orchestrated, leaves out an information entry whose url one of those blocks already lists (FR-28), adds one block per entry file made by `render.py` `entry` (lines 126-174) fed from the file, and builds the shared page `.dev/coordinator/report.html` with the builder of `page.py`. For such a block `entry` takes every written part, `happened` and each session's status from the file, because its unchanged branch otherwise prints "Nothing since the previous report" (lines 136-140) and replaces every session status with `carried_status` (lines 146-149), which keeps only a status that starts with `waiting on you` or `done` and turns every other, `working` included, into `stalled`. The daily run's own page build writes the shared page too (`page.py:198-199`, "the one place it lives"), so without a step after it every orchestrated issue would vanish from the page each morning: Step 12 therefore ends with `run.py refresh --run <run>`, which is allowed while that run holds the run lock, builds the shared page from that run's `overview.md` plus the entry files and never writes `<run>/report.html`, so `run.py gate` (which checks that file against the sha256 of `overview.md`) is unchanged. This is the smaller change: `page.py` and `run.py gate` are not edited, one command is added to Step 12, and `refresh` is the only builder of the page that carries entries. Called without `--run`, `refresh` (run by the orchestrator at each milestone) does nothing while a daily run holds the lock and is retried at the next milestone. Section 6.4 lists every reader and what each prints. The two existing automations keep their names (`run.py open` copies run history only for names starting `coordinator-`, SKILL.md:26) and are re-pointed with `orca automations edit` at the installed script path; the new poll automation is named `orchestrator-poll` so the history copy ignores it | The report scripts already carry item text between runs by item URL; an entry file in that shape reuses them with no new store, and removing held items before comparison is smaller than teaching four readers to skip them. Keeping the names keeps the run-history copy working | `common.py:19-26`, `:205-208`, `:244-255`; `config.json`; `SKILL.md:26` (IronFlow `agent-work-report`); `orca automations list`; accuracy-check row 27 | decided (OD6; the `refresh` verb, `exclude.py` and the ClickUp re-homing are UNVERIFIED, section 12) |
| DQ13 | How "issues that could be assigned to the user" and "issues and pull requests that affect the user's work" become report entries | They are information only: never claimed, never given a Run or a worker. Wave 0 writes one information entry for each into `.dev/coordinator/orchestrated/info/<kind>__<owner>__<repo>__<N>.json` in the same slot format, state `not-started`, owner "none", under the report section "Watching", with the link, the kind (`could_assign`, `mention`, `affects`), the repo and the time last seen. An entry is dropped when the issue or pull request closes or after 14 days without change (`info_ttl_days`). The same item found again by the daily run (mentions, FR-3) merges by URL (FR-28), never twice | The issue says only assignment triggers a claim and the other three are "passed on as information"; the report is the place to see them | issue 655 section 1 step 2 and section 7; FR-28 (one item across sources) | no |

No `plugins/superclaude/` copy is added: that folder holds neither `issue.md` nor `pr-submit.md` (the plugin mirror is selective, `ls plugins/superclaude/commands`, E155) | It follows `sc-issue-protocol` (`name: sc:issue-protocol`) and the other two model skills; no command or folder with this name exists on `origin/master` or `origin/integration` | `sc-issue-protocol/SKILL.md:2`; accuracy-check row 30; `ls ~/.claude/skills` (no orchestrator folder) | no |
| OQ6 | Is assignment really a gate (ruling R2, "triage or write access")? | Yes for organization repos, with two gaps. GitHub's repository-roles table lists "assign all issues and pull requests" for Triage and above and marks "have an issue assigned to them" for every role including Read; the assignment guide says "anyone with write access to a repository can assign"; the REST page says push access. The three pages differ on the minimum (triage or write) but all exclude read-only users from assigning others. Gaps: (1) the guide lists "your own account" among assignable people, so whether a read-only user can self-assign is UNVERIFIED; (2) apps and bots with issue-write permission can assign. The repo list is the allow list that contains both gaps: an issue in an unlisted repo is never claimed, and the day cap bounds a burst | The owner stated R2; the documents back it with the stated differences | GitHub "Repository roles for an organization" (matrix, "Triage" row: "assign all issues and pull requests"); "Assigning issues and pull requests to other GitHub users" ("Anyone with write access to a repository can assign issues and pull requests"; eligible: own account, previous commenters, users with write permissions, organization members with read permissions; up to 10 assignees); REST "Issue assignees" (push access); evidence E151 | no |

The handoff worker runs `/sc:pr-submit --monitor 3`, but the contract lists `/sc:pr-submit` as never-as-worker (`WORKER-CONTRACT.md:23`) and says PRs go only through it "run in an ordinary top-level session as decision D3 states, never as a worker" (`:244`); WC-9 switches every push, PR and GitHub post off for a worker and no brief can turn them on; the coordinator section says the coordinator acts outward only when the user's instruction names the action and confirms with the person, and pushes worker commits only on the person's go (`:239-246`); the coordinator is "a session the person opened". The autonomous merge, close and comments of ruling R10 contradict all of this. Decision: PR B adds one section to `WORKER-CONTRACT.md`, "Orchestrated issue flow", with its test change in `tests/skills/test_worker_contract.py`. It defines an orchestrated-issue worker (brief line `Flow: orchestrated-issue`) that may push its own feature branch and open its pull request through `/sc:pr-submit --monitor 3` in its own top-level session (owner ruling 3, 2026-11-19), must never merge, delete a branch or force push (only the orchestrator merges), and runs no `--unattended` command line for `/sc:pr-submit` (it has no unattended mode, `grep unattended pr-submit.md` finds none). It defines the orchestrator as a standing-grant coordinator that may be started by a script, whose outward actions are exactly the `grants` list (DQ6). Everything else in the contract is unchanged | Without this, the first worker either breaks the contract or cannot open its pull request; the issue says only that the handoff "must also satisfy the brief layout" | `WORKER-CONTRACT.md:23`, `:44-47`, `:239-246`; accuracy-check row 32; `pr-submit.md` (no unattended section) | decided (OD1) |
| ADD2 | Where the claim runs | `/sc:issue --claim` takes its repo from `origin` of the current directory only and writes `.dev/issues/<N>/` there; the orchestrator session runs in the orchestrator repo. Decision: wave 1 runs the claim with the current directory set to the issue's claim checkout (the checkout of that repo from the repo-to-checkout map, on any branch, because the claim reads only `origin` and writes only the untracked `.dev/issues/<N>/`; no new worktree is made for it, which also keeps the CLAUDE.md rule that every worktree gets an agent) and prefixes every shell step with `cd <checkout> &&`, as the hand-run of issue 655 had to (PROCESS-LOG entry 2). The claim's files (`.dev/issues/<N>/`) stay in that checkout, are never staged, and wave 4 passes them to the worker with `orca_worktree.py worktree --source`. Whether `/sc:issue` keeps that directory for all its steps is UNVERIFIED and is the first local end-to-end check | A `--repo` flag does not exist and the claim forbids one (B10); changing the claim is outside this issue | `sc-issue-protocol/SKILL.md:72` (origin only, no `--repo`), `:77`, `:82`; `origin_repo.py` docstring; PROCESS-LOG.md entry 2 | no |
| ADD3 | How the poll finds assigned issues across 65 repos in two orgs within its call budget | One GitHub search per (login, org): `is:issue is:open assignee:<login> org:<owner>`; the repo list then filters the results. The three information streams are one search each per org (mentions, unassigned new issues, involvement), limited to items updated since the last poll. About 8 calls per cycle against the budget of 35 (`CALL_CAP`); a per-repo list would need up to 65 calls. A result with 100 or more items fails closed as `truncated` (as `DISCOVERY_BOUND` does today). The search API has its own limit of 30 requests a minute and a short indexing delay, so an assignment can appear one poll late. The S1 reply shape was observed with one read-only call on 2026-11-19: `total_count` 17, `incomplete_results: false`, each item with `repository_url` (`https://api.github.com/repos/<owner>/<repo>`), `assignees[].login` and no `pull_request` key, and `rate_limit` shows `search` limit 30; the index delay and the replies of S2 to S4 are UNVERIFIED (U8) and the live test captures them | The built `argv_discover` lists one repo at a time (`github.py:180-199`, `REPO` at `:37`) | `github.py:37`, `:40-49`, `:180-199`, `:486-488`; `.dev/orchestrator-655/spec/org-repos-*.tsv` (48 + 17 repos) | no |
| ADD4 | Merging in a repo where a review agent does not run or does not finish | The merge condition is that every review agent the repo lists in `reviewers` of `repos.yaml` (`augment`, `codex`, or both) has finished on the final head. If one of them shows no activity on the final head, or started and has not finished, 60 minutes after the head's checks finished, the orchestrator holds the pull request and escalates to the human under rule 7 (a stall it cannot unstick); it never merges without the review (owner ruling 6, 2026-11-19). `reviewers: none` is the one way a repo has no review agent to wait for, and the owner sets it. The value `detect` of the earlier draft is dropped: an agent that never showed activity is no longer skipped as `/sc:pr-submit` skips it, because the ruling makes that a hold | `merge_wait` returns `no_response` when both are absent (`reviewers.py:395-427`), which this design now maps to a hold; the review comments on recent merged pull requests show Augment (`augmentcode`) and Codex (`chatgpt-codex-connector`) on IronClaude #641, Augment alone on IronFlow #1 and #3, Codex alone on IronOrca #11 and none on #8 (`gh pr view <N> --json reviews,comments`, 2026-11-19), so the lists of 4.4 are a reading of three repos, UNVERIFIED as the permanent set | `reviewers.py:395-427` (`merge_wait`); `CLAUDE.md` (a reviewer with no activity is skipped) | decided (OD4) |
| ADD5 | What `gh pr merge` may carry | Never `--auto` (it can queue a merge for later when checks are not green, `gh pr merge --help` line 7), never `--admin` (it bypasses requirements), never `--delete-branch` (deleting branches is human-only, rule 1). Always `--repo <R>`, the method of the repo and `--match-head-commit <sha>` | These three flags each break a merge condition or a ruling | `gh pr merge --help`; issue 655 section 9 rule 1; CLAUDE.md fork rule (every `gh` call pinned) | no |

The model runs them and reads their JSON; it writes only language (summaries, escalations, entries). ## 3. The wake message

PR A (the poll script) writes this format and PR B (the orchestrator skill, wave 0) parses it. Both build against this section and nothing else; a change here is a change to both. Every wake is addressed to exactly one orchestrator (5.7): a cycle that routes issues to two orchestrators writes two wakes, each with its own wake id and its own three artifacts below.

### 3.1 Three artifacts, one wake id

| Artifact | Written by | Holds | Purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| Wake file `<orchestrator-repo>/.orchestrator/wakes/<wake_id>.json` | the poll script | every fact (section 3.4) | the one source of truth for a wake |
| Mailbox message to `run:<run id>` | the poll script, after the file exists | the wake id only | reaches a session that is busy or blocked in `check --wait` |
| Typed line `/sc:orchestrator --wake <wake_id>` | the poll script, only when the session is idle | the wake id only | starts a turn in an idle session |

`wake_id` matches `^[0-9]{8}T[0-9]{6}Z-[0-9a-f]{8}$`: the UTC time of the cycle's first GitHub read and 8 hex digits from the system random source. It is generated by the poll script and holds no text from GitHub. The typed line, the mailbox subject and the file name carry nothing else, so no title, label or comment of an issue can reach a typed line: this is the carried-over "only a validated value reaches the launch prompt" rule in its new form (section 7.1).

### 3.2 The typed line

Exactly: `/sc:orchestrator --wake ` followed by the wake id, no other character, at most 64 bytes. The poll builds it with one function (`build_wake_line`) that takes a string, checks it against the wake id pattern, and refuses any other input, the way `build_claim_prompt` refuses a non-integer today (`launch.py:84-92`, pattern `PROMPT_PATTERN` at `:33`). It sends it with `orca terminal send --terminal <handle> --text <line> --enter --wait-submit 15` after the checks of DQ2 (idle, no draft, no output in the last 60 seconds). It records the receipt stages (`input_accepted`, `turn_started`) in the audit log.

### 3.3 The mailbox message

`orca orchestration send --to run:<run id of the target orchestrator> --type status --subject "orchestrator-wake <wake_id>" --payload '{"kind":"orchestrator-wake","wake_id":"<wake_id>"}'`. The orchestrator tells it from a worker's `status` acknowledgment (WC-12) by the subject prefix `orchestrator-wake ` and the payload `kind`. That a sender outside any Run may post to `run:<id>` is the documented rule (`orchestration send --help`: "a sender in no Run must use run:<id> or dispatch:<id>") but no one has run it from a plain shell: UNVERIFIED (section 12, U5). If it fails, the poll falls back to the typed line alone and records `mailbox_unavailable`.

### 3.4 The wake file

JSON, UTF-8, at most 64 KiB, written with temp file plus `rename` and mode 0600 before anything is sent. Times are UTC `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ`. Every string except the closed tokens is validated as below; nothing in the file is an instruction.

| Field | Type and rule |
|---|---|
| `schema` | the string `orchestrator-wake/1` |
| `target` | an object with the four fields `target.orchestrator`, `target.tag`, `target.repo` and `target.resume`, described below |
| `wake_id` | the id of section 3.1; must equal the file name |
| `created_at` | time of the first GitHub read of the cycle, from the server `Date` header (the poll already reads it, `cycle.py:770-775`) |
| `poll.cycle_id`, `poll.host` | the cycle's id and host name, as in the audit log |
| `poll.login` | the user's login (pattern `[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9-]{0,38}`, the cycle's `_LOGIN`) |
| `target.orchestrator`, `target.tag`, `target.repo` | the one orchestrator this wake is for: its id (`<short>-<n>`), its session tag (`orchestrator-<short>-<n>`) and its repo (`owner/name`, a member of the repo list); every `assigned[]` item has this repo |
| `target.resume` | boolean; true only in a wake that restarts a dead orchestrator (5.7 point 5), which then has no `assigned[]` items |
| `poll.since` | the time after which the information streams were read |
| `poll.gh_calls` | integer, calls used (`CallBudget.total`) |
| `poll.dry_run` | boolean; a dry run writes no file (section 4.9), so this is always `false` in a file that exists |
| `attempt` | integer 1 to 3, the delivery attempt of this wake |
| `assigned[]` (0 to 10) | `repo` (`owner/name`, pattern `^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9-]{0,38}/[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,100}$` and a member of the repo list), `number` (integer 1 to 999999999, never a boolean or string), `assignees` (logins, same pattern, at most 10), `first_seen_at`, `checkout_state` (`ok`, `missing` or `origin_mismatch`; the poll's per-repo origin check) |
| `info.mentions[]`, `info.could_assign[]`, `info.affects[]` (0 to 50 each) | `repo`, `number`, `kind` (`issue` or `pull_request`), `updated_at` |
| `plan_requests[]` | one item per plan request (4.11 point 5): `repo`, `number`, `title`, `url`, `missing` (a list of the closed tokens `priority`, `fix_approach`, `test_plan`), `held_since`, `requested_at`; the same patterns as an `assigned[]` item (3.7 point 2) |
| `skipped[]` (0 to 100) | `repo`, `number`, `reason` from the closed list of section 4.7 (for example `repo_not_listed`, `repo_disabled`, `assigned_other`, `day_cap`) |

The `assigned[]` row gets no field for the hold: an issue in `assigned[]` has passed the check, because the poll never sends a held issue as an assignment (ruling 23). The array `plan_requests[]` stands beside `assigned[]`. A wake file that holds only `plan_requests[]` is a wake like any other for delivery, targeting and acknowledgment (3.5, 3.6); in the acknowledgment each request is in exactly one of `taken` and `skipped` (`already_planned`, `held_backlog`, `not_open`); `taken` is written at the START of the plan call and only after the plan record of the request exists (4.11 point 5), and the end of the call is not a second acknowledgment but the status of that plan record `.orchestrator/issues/plan-<key>.<requested_at>.json` (`done` or `failed`), which the poll reads. In the `skipped[]` row the reasons come from the closed list of 4.7, which now holds the eight hold reasons. No other change: the schema stays `orchestrator-wake/1`, because PR A and PR B are not yet built and both build to this text.

Not in the file, on purpose: titles, bodies, labels, comment text, the checkout path (the orchestrator reads `repos.yaml` itself, so there is one truth) and the merge settings.

Example (one new issue, one mention, one skipped):

```json
{
  "schema": "orchestrator-wake/1",
  "wake_id": "20261119T101500Z-3fa9c1d2",
  "created_at": "2026-11-19T10:15:00Z",
  "poll": {"cycle_id": "c-20261119T101500Z", "host": "ws-aidev02", "login": "clayphi",
           "since": "2026-11-19T10:10:00Z", "gh_calls": 9, "dry_run": false},
  "target": {"orchestrator": "ironclaude-1", "tag": "orchestrator-ironclaude-1",
             "repo": "IronbellyOrg/IronClaude", "resume": false},
  "attempt": 1,
  "assigned": [{"repo": "IronbellyOrg/IronClaude", "number": 655, "assignees": ["clayphi"],
                "first_seen_at": "2026-11-19T10:15:00Z", "checkout_state": "ok"}],
  "info": {"mentions": [{"repo": "IronbellyGroup/example", "number": 12, "kind": "issue",
                         "updated_at": "2026-11-19T10:12:41Z"}],
           "could_assign": [], "affects": []},
  "skipped": [{"repo": "IronbellyOrg/Coder", "number": 477, "reason": "repo_not_listed"}]
}
```

### 3.5 Who parses it and how a malformed wake is handled

Wave 0 runs `superclaude orchestrator wake-check <wake_id>` (decision ADD6), which reads the file, applies every rule above and prints the normalized JSON or a refusal. Outcomes:

| Case | Handling |
|---|---|
| File missing, unreadable, over 64 KiB, not JSON, `schema` not `orchestrator-wake/1` (including a future `/2`), or `wake_id` different from the file name | The whole wake is rejected: write `<wake_id>.rejected.json` with a closed reason (`missing`, `too_large`, `not_json`, `schema_unsupported`, `id_mismatch`), take nothing, send no acknowledgment, and treat it as a failed handoff (escalation rule 8). The poll sees no acknowledgment and sends the same issues again as a new wake, up to attempt 3 (DQ2) |
| `target.orchestrator` is not the id of the orchestrator that reads the wake, or `target.tag` does not match its tag | The whole wake is rejected with the closed reason `wrong_target`: `<wake_id>.rejected.json` is written, nothing is taken and no acknowledgment is sent; the poll resends it to its target (DQ2), never to this orchestrator |
| One item fails its rule (bad repo or login pattern, number not an integer in range, unknown stream or kind) | Only that item is dropped. It is listed in the acknowledgment under `rejected_items` with reason `malformed_item`; the rest is processed |
| An `assigned` repo is not in the orchestrator's own `repos.yaml` read (the file changed since the poll) or is disabled | The item is skipped with `repo_not_listed` or `repo_disabled` and appears in the acknowledgment |
| Unknown extra keys | Ignored; named in the acknowledgment under `warnings` |
| The wake id was already acknowledged | The same acknowledgment is written again (identical content) and nothing is processed twice |
| An `assigned` item already has a per-issue state file with status `in_progress` or a final status | Not taken again; listed under `skipped` with reason `already_dispatched`; an in-progress file is resumed by wave 0's restart rule (section 5.5) |

### 3.6 The acknowledgment

When wave 0 has decided every item, it writes `<orchestrator-repo>/.orchestrator/wakes/<wake_id>.ack.json` with temp file plus `rename`: `{"schema":"orchestrator-wake-ack/1","wake_id":...,"acked_at":...,"orchestrator":{"id":...,"run_id":...,"host":...},"taken":[{"repo","number"}],"skipped":[{"repo","number","reason"}],"rejected_items":[{"index","reason"}],"warnings":[...]}`. Every `assigned` item is in exactly one of `taken`, `skipped` or `rejected_items`; every `plan_requests[]` item is in exactly one of `taken` and `skipped` (the plan request, 3.4). At its next cycle the poll reads each acknowledgment: `taken` and `skipped` entries move to the state `acked` (a skip reason that cannot change, such as `claimed_elsewhere` or `repo_not_listed`, is recorded and the issue is not woken again), and an entry in none of the lists stays `woken` and is sent again when its 10 minutes pass. After the acknowledgment is read the poll moves the wake file and the acknowledgment to `wakes/done/` and never deletes them.

### 3.7 Guarantees the two PRs must keep

1. At-least-once delivery of an assignment to the orchestrator, or a loud failure (after 3 unacknowledged attempts, or 3 failed preparations, the cycle exits 7 and the workspace is marked unread; 4.3 rule 4); at-most-once claim and at-most-once `worker-start` per issue, which the orchestrator enforces (DQ3, section 5.5), not the poll.
2. No text from GitHub reaches a typed line, a mailbox subject or a shell command line without a pattern check; every `gh` and `orca` argument built from a wake field is checked against the same patterns in the code that builds it.
3. The poll never writes `.ack.json` or `.rejected.json`; the orchestrator never writes the wake file.
4. A wake file with `poll.dry_run` true is never written, so a shadow run cannot cause a claim.


## 4. The poll script

### 4.1 What changes in one paragraph

The built poller finds issues carrying a trigger label and an approval label from an approver, on one repo, and starts a claim session through `orca agent launch` (`cycle.py:915-961`, `launch.py:95-116`). The reshaped poll finds issues assigned to the user in any listed repo, decides which are new, and delivers them to the orchestrator session as a wake (section 3). It claims nothing and starts no worker; its only GitHub writes are adding and removing the hold label (4.11; no comment, ruling 33). Everything else the built poller does for safety stays (section 7.1).

### 4.2 Discovery: assignment only, plus three information streams

All reads are `gh api search/issues` calls pinned by an `org:<owner>` qualifier, counted by `CallBudget`, with `per_page=100`. A repo outside the two orgs of `repos.yaml` is never queried, and every result row is checked again against the repo list (decision ADD3).

| Stream | Search query (per org, per login where noted) | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| S1 assigned (the only trigger) | `is:issue is:open assignee:<login> org:<owner>`, once per org for the user's login | candidates for a claim, after the filters of 4.3 |
| S2 mentions | `mentions:<login> updated:>=<since> org:<owner>` | information only |
| S3 could be assigned | `is:issue is:open no:assignee created:>=<since> org:<owner>`, kept only for repos in the list | information only |
| S4 affects the user's work | `involves:<login> updated:>=<since> org:<owner> -assignee:<login>` | information only |

About 8 calls a cycle for two orgs (budget 35). A result with 100 or more items is `truncated` and fails closed for S1; for S2 to S4 it is a warning and the stream is cut at 100. `incomplete_results: true` in a reply is treated like a malformed reply (`gh_non_json`, retried next cycle). The labels of an S1 row are read only to find `hold:plan` and `hold:backlog` (4.11); no board column and no Project is read anywhere (R1, R3). Only S1 can lead to a claim.

### 4.3 The new-since-last-poll rule

1. The store key is `owner/repo#number` (a string; today it is an integer issue number, `record.py:132`, `:159-166`).
2. An S1 issue is new when the store has no entry for its key. A key whose entry is `acked` is never woken again by the poll; an entry that is `pending`, `woken` or `unknown` with no acknowledgment is resent under rule 4; an `abandoned` entry was reported loudly (rule 4) and is not woken again. A reopened or re-assigned issue the poll has seen before is taken with `/sc:orchestrator --take <owner/repo#N>` (section 5.1), which writes a synthetic wake. An issue held by 4.11 has no store entry; it is new in the first cycle it is clear. A plan request is not a wake of the issue: its delivery state is the field `plan_request` of the issue's `holds` entry, and it is delivered, acknowledged and re-delivered like a wake (rule 4).
3. S2 to S4 items are new when their key `(stream, repo, number, updated_at)` is not in `info_seen` (the 500 newest keys, kept in the store). `since` is the `created_at` of the previous completed cycle minus 5 minutes of overlap; skew is tolerated because the time comes from the server `Date` header (`cycle.py:770-775`).
4. One exception to "send only what is new" (ruling R7): an issue whose wake was delivered but not acknowledged in 10 minutes, or whose entry a cycle that died left `pending`, or whose send outcome was `unknown`, is sent again in a new wake, at most twice more (3 attempts in all). This is a re-delivery of a lost message, not new data. An issue still unacknowledged after the third attempt becomes `abandoned` with `ack_not_observed`, and an issue the poll could not prepare three times in a row becomes `abandoned` with `attempts_exhausted`; each of the two makes the cycle exit 7 and marks the orchestrator workspace unread (DQ2), so an assignment is never dropped silently.
5. First cycle after `init-state`: the poll records every issue already assigned, and every item already visible in S2 to S4, as seen without waking anyone (a baseline), unless `init-state --no-baseline` is given. This is decision OD7 (owner ruling 9, 2026-11-19: baseline); without it, switching on would wake every issue already assigned to the user (a read-only search on 2026-11-19 finds 17 open issues assigned to the user in IronbellyOrg, 8 in IronClaude, 5 in IronOrca, 3 in Coder and 1 in Ironeye, and none in IronbellyGroup; evidence E152), limited only by the day cap. Step 7a never writes on an issue recorded at the baseline.
6. Nothing new and nothing to resend: no wake file, no mailbox message, no typed line, no session start; the cycle writes its audit lines and exits 0. No model runs.

### 4.4 The repo list and the repo-to-checkout map

They live in `<orchestrator-repo>/repos.yaml`, never in IronClaude (ruling R15); the repo list is its `repos` array and there is no second file. The poll's own configuration file in IronClaude (`~/.config/superclaude/issue-watch.json`, `cli.py:30`) keeps only: `enabled`, `orchestrator_repo` (absolute path), `owner_account_id`, `state_dir` and `session_tag_prefix` (default `orchestrator`; the full tag of an orchestrator is `<prefix>-<short>-<n>`, 5.7).

Minimum format of `repos.yaml` (`schema_version` 1; the orchestrator owns the file; the poll reads the fields marked P and ignores the rest):

```yaml
schema_version: 1
orgs: [IronbellyOrg, IronbellyGroup]
repos:
  - repo: IronbellyOrg/IronClaude
    enabled: true
    checkout: /abs/path/to/clone
    clone_parent: /abs/parent
    default_branch: master
    merge_method: squash
    release_merge_method: merge
    release_head: integration
    protected: false
    can_merge: true
    reviewers: [augment, codex]
    merge_safety_files: [".github/workflows/**", ".github/rulesets/**", ".github/settings.yml", "CODEOWNERS", ".github/CODEOWNERS", "docs/CODEOWNERS", ".claude/settings.json", "src/superclaude/hooks/**", "plugins/superclaude/hooks/**"]
    max_in_flight: 3
    overview: overviews/IronbellyOrg__IronClaude.md
grants: [issue.assign, issue.comment, issue.body.append_plan_sections, issue.hold, pr.merge, issue.close]
plan_tier: tier1b
worker_tier: tier2
orchestrator_tier: tier1b
info_ttl_days: 14
```

Parser: the poll script reads the file with `yaml.safe_load` from PyYAML, never `yaml.load`. PyYAML is already a runtime dependency of IronClaude (`pyproject.toml` line 38, `"pyyaml>=6.0"`, in the main `dependencies` list, not an extra), and `import yaml` with `safe_load` is already used in `src/superclaude/cli/sweep/probes.py`, `cli/eval/loader.py` and others, so no dependency is added. Validation rejects a file whose top level is not a mapping as `repos_invalid`. The file has no `working_hours` key: the hours of the worker check are fixed by owner ruling 7 (DQ10).

P fields: `orgs`, `grants` (the poll writes the hold label under `issue.hold`; no grant, no write), `orchestrator_tier` and, for each repo, `repo`, `enabled`, `checkout`, `orchestrator_tier`. Validation (`check-config` and every cycle): `schema_version` is 1; each `repo` matches the repo pattern of section 3.4 and its owner is in `orgs`; `checkout` is null or an absolute path; `merge_method` and `release_merge_method` (when present) are `squash`, `merge` or `rebase`; `release_head` is a branch name or null (a null `release_head` means the repo has no release pull request and `merge_method` always applies); `reviewers` is `none` or a non-empty list of `augment` and `codex`; `merge_safety_files` (when present) is a list of non-empty strings, each a glob pattern (an empty list is valid and means no hold, and is written only on Clay's recorded word; the default list when the key is absent is the one of 5.3 wave 7); `max_in_flight` is an integer from 1 to 10 (default 3 when absent); `plan_tier`, `worker_tier` and `orchestrator_tier`, at the top level and in a repo's entry, are tier names that `ccsession --help` lists (5.8); `plan_effort` and `worker_effort` are non-empty strings; `reach_daily_max` is an integer from 0 to 50 (any other value is read as 0). A failure stops the cycle with `repos_invalid` (exit 2), never a guess.

Who keeps it current: the orchestrator, through `superclaude orchestrator repos` verbs (decision ADD6), on the user's word in the session or on a "Needs you" proposal the orchestrator makes once when an issue shows up in an unlisted repo (the FR-20 pattern, no hand editing). `add` reads `permissions`, `default_branch`, the allowed merge methods and `protected` from `gh api repos/<R>` and sets `can_merge`; `refresh` repeats that read for every repo (weekly, and on request); the overview file `overviews/<owner>__<repo>.md` is written by the orchestrator from the repo's README and its `CLAUDE.md` when a repo is added and updated when the user says so. A repo where the user has only read access is stored with `enabled: false` and `can_merge: false`.

Per-repo origin check: before an issue of a repo is put in a wake, `git -C <checkout> remote get-url origin` must name that same `owner/repo`, compared case-insensitively, the check `check_origin` makes today against one constant (`launch.py:233-252`, `ORIGIN_REPO` at `:50`). The expected repo becomes a parameter. A missing checkout is `checkout_state: missing` in the wake (the orchestrator then applies DQ4); a mismatch is `origin_mismatch` and the issue is skipped. No remote of a fork's upstream is read or written: every query names `org:<owner>` of a listed org and every row is re-checked against the list.

### 4.5 The reshaped cycle

1. Stop `not_enabled` unless `--dry-run`; read the poll configuration and `repos.yaml` (`repos_invalid` or `orchestrator_repo_invalid`, exit 2).
2. Take the store lock (`lock_held`, exit 75, when another cycle holds it).
3. Load the store; stop `rate_limited` while `next_permitted_at` is in the future.
4. Read `gh api user`; check the login pattern and `owner_account_id`; note the server time and the clock skew.
5. Read the acknowledgments of earlier wakes (section 3.6) and move entries.
6. Sweep: an entry still `pending` at the start of a cycle was left by a cycle that died between steps 10 and 11 (the lock is held for a whole cycle): it is recorded as `pending_orphan` and queued for resend; a `woken` or `unknown` entry past 10 minutes is queued for resend; an entry queued with its 3 attempts already used becomes `abandoned` with `ack_not_observed` (exit 7 and the unread mark). A plan request that is `taken` and whose plan record (the one named by its `plan_requested_at`) is still `in_progress` or does not exist `PLAN_CALL_MAX_MINUTES` (60) after `plan_acked_at` raises `plan_stuck` (exit 7, unread mark) and is set to `plan_request: stuck`, which ends its count as a place; so it is raised once.
7. Run S1 to S4 (4.2).
7a. Hold step (4.11): for each S1 issue as in 4.11 point 3, skip, apply, release or add a plan request to the wake; issues that remain go on to step 8.
8. Filter S1: repo in the list and enabled, assignee rule (DQ1), no store entry (4.3), day cap (`DAY_CAP` 6 new issues in 24 hours; the day cap counts a plan request when it is sent), at most `MAX_CANDIDATES` 10 in number order.
9. Re-read each candidate (`argv_reread`, repo-pinned): still open, still assigned to the user, and the check still passes (an assignment) or still fails and the label is still there (a plan request). Then the per-repo origin check.
10. Route (5.7): under `.orchestrator/state.lock`, read the registry and the state files, count each orchestrator's issues in flight, and give each candidate, repo by repo in number order, to the first `active` orchestrator of its repo with fewer than `max_in_flight`, or to a new orchestrator `<short>-<n>` added to the registry. Write each candidate's entry as `pending` with `orchestrator: <id>` and save, before anything is sent (the built rule: durable before the act, `cycle.py:1090-1092`). A plan request is routed after all assignments of the cycle and only to an `active` orchestrator of the repo with a free place (an open plan request counts as a place, 5.7 point 1); when none has one, nothing is sent and `plan_requested_at` is not set, and the next cycle tries again; a new orchestrator is added for a plan request only when the repo has no `active` orchestrator. Write one wake file per orchestrator that has routed candidates, and release the lock.
11. For each wake, send the mailbox message to that orchestrator's Run and type the line when its session is idle. Session missing or dead: start it as `waker.py` does (`terminal create` then typing `ccsession orchestrator-<short>-<n> --tier <orchestrator_tier>`, `waker.py:144-169`; owner ruling 4, 2026-11-19; 5.8). Mark entries `woken`, or `unknown` when the send outcome is unknown (then they are resent like an unacknowledged wake). Also, for an `active` orchestrator in the registry whose terminal is gone while it has `in_progress` state files, start it and send a `target.resume` wake (5.7 point 5).
12. Commit the store once, write the audit lines, print `cycle:` and `exit:`, exit with the code of `resolve_exit` (precedence 2, 6, 7, 5, 3, 4; 75 alone; else 0, `cycle.py:460-478`).

The built deadline (240 s in all, 120 s cut-off before any send, 20 s per `gh` call, `cycle.py:37-47`) stays. The poll runs detached from its automation (4.8), so the 60-second precheck limit does not apply to it.

### 4.6 Backoff, budget, locking

- Rate limits: with a `Retry-After` or reset header the poll waits until that time, capped at 3600 s (`rate_limit_wait`, `github.py:418`). With no header it now waits `min(3600, 300 x 2^streak)` seconds, where `streak` counts consecutive rate-limited cycles and is stored (schema 2) and reset by the first cycle that finishes with no rate limit. Today it clamps to a flat 3600 s (`cycle.py:663-668`); this change is ruling R6.
- An unrecognized GitHub error text is treated as a login failure on the shared reads (`gh_auth_failed`, exit 3) until the live test fixes `GH_ERROR_SIGNS` (`github.py:269-275`).
- Call budget: `CALL_CAP` 35 stays (`github.py:46`); `RESERVED_CALLS` (3 today, `:49`) becomes the candidate cap plus 2, because every candidate is re-read once.
- Lock: the `flock` on `state_dir/lock` stays (`record.py:282`); a second cycle exits 75, which the detached automation hides, so `status` and the audit log are where it is seen.
- `state_dir` on a network file system is refused at load (`cycle.py:50`, `:280-283`, follow-up FU-12 stays open).

### 4.7 Reason table after the change

`REASON_TABLE` has 59 reasons today (`audit.py:26-102`). It stays a closed table with one test that asserts its exact contents.

- Removed (14): `approvers_invalid`, `worktree_invalid`, `project_read_failed`, `not_approved`, `approval_before_trigger`, `approval_before_init`, `events_incomplete`, `events_unreadable`, `edited_after_approval`, `project_mismatch`, `project_unreadable`, `edits_unreadable`, `marker_unreadable`, and the note `re-approved`.
- Renamed (5): `launched` to `woken`, `claimed` to `acked`, `claim_not_observed` to `ack_not_observed` (its exit code changes from 0 to 7: a wake nobody acknowledged is a condition the owner must see), `launch_unknown` to `wake_unknown`, `prompt_invalid` to `wake_line_invalid`.
- Added (8): `repos_invalid` (cycle, exit 2), `orchestrator_repo_invalid` (cycle, exit 2), `repo_not_listed`, `repo_disabled`, `checkout_missing` (skip, exit 0), `no_session` (entry, exit 7), `wake_write_failed` (cycle, exit 6), `mailbox_unavailable` (warning).
- Changed meaning (2): `pending_orphan` keeps kind entry and exit 0 but now means "resend", not "abandon" (4.5 step 6); `attempts_exhausted` changes from exit 0 to exit 7, because an issue the poll could not prepare after 3 tries is a condition the owner must see.
- Added by the hold (8, after the 8 already added): `held_incomplete` (skip, exit 0), `held_backlog` (skip, exit 0), `hold_removed_by_hand` (skip, exit 0), `hold_applied` (note, exit 0), `hold_released` (note, exit 0), `plan_requested` (note, exit 0), `hold_write_failed` (warning, exit 0) and `plan_stuck` (a cycle reason with exit 7, like `abandoned`: a plan call that is `taken` and still not finished after `PLAN_CALL_MAX_MINUTES`, 4.11 point 5).
- Total after: 62 (54 plus the 8 of the hold; the 54 are the 8 added above plus `registry_invalid`, a cycle reason with exit 2: the registry `.orchestrator/orchestrators.json` is unreadable or malformed, 5.7). Every other reason keeps its name, kind and exit code.

### 4.8 The Orca automation

One automation, created disabled and enabled at switch-on (ruling R4; no system cron entry exists or is created):

```
orca automations create --name orchestrator-poll --trigger "*/5 * * * *" --provider claude \
  --prompt "unused: the precheck never lets a run start" --workspace path:<orchestrator-repo> \
  --precheck "bash -lc 'set -a; [ -f ~/.aienv ] && . ~/.aienv; [ -f ~/.env ] && . ~/.env; set +a; setsid nohup superclaude issue-watch once >> <state_dir>/poll.log 2>&1 < /dev/null &'; exit 1" \
  --disabled
```

The precheck is the pattern of `waker.py` (evidence E130): it starts the poll detached and exits 1, so Orca records `skipped_precheck`, starts no agent and leaves no terminal; the poll's own exit code is in `audit.jsonl` and `superclaude issue-watch status`, not in Orca's run history. `--prompt` and `--provider` are required by the command but never used. Orca's scheduler ignores time zones (`waker.py:37-39`); a 5-minute trigger does not depend on one. "Run now" and `orca automations run` bypass the precheck (`automations create --help`), so the shadow run is a scheduled one. UNVERIFIED: that `superclaude` is on the PATH of `bash -lc` in the automation's environment, and that a `*/5` cron string is accepted by this Orca version (section 12, U6, U7). The shadow run puts `--dry-run` in the command; switch-on edits it out with `orca automations edit`.

### 4.9 Dry-run

`once --dry-run` reads GitHub, applies every rule of 4.2 to 4.5, writes audit lines marked `dry_run: true` with the reason `woken` as "would wake" and a `would_wake` list on the cycle result, and writes nothing else: no wake file, no mailbox message, no typed line, no session start, no `dispatch.json` change. Its shadow memory (`dry-run.json`, `record.py:805-851`) keeps what it would have woken so the 24-hour shadow run can be read as a whole. A dry run ignores `enabled` (today's rule, `cycle.py:685`).


### 4.10 Module by module: keep, reshape or remove

Lines are in `src/superclaude/cli/issue_watch/` on `origin/master` 89dc403d4 (read for this design). "Reshape" means the name stays and the body changes; PR A does all of this. Where a removed symbol might have another user, PR A greps for it first (`codegraph_callers` or Grep) and keeps it if one is found.

| File | Symbol (line) | Disposition | Change |
|---|---|---|---|
| `cycle.py` | `MAX_CANDIDATES` (34), `GH_TIMEOUT_S`, `PROBE_TIMEOUT_S`, `GIT_TIMEOUT_S`, `LAUNCH_TIMEOUT_S`, `DEADLINE_TOTAL_S`, `GH_FLOOR_S`, `LAUNCH_CUTOFF_S`, `SKEW_WARN_S`, `HEALTH_AGE_S`, `STALE_AGE_S`, `DISCOVERY_WARN` (37-47), `NETWORK_FS_TYPES` (50) | keep | `LAUNCH_TIMEOUT_S` and `LAUNCH_CUTOFF_S` now bound the session start and the wake send |
| `cycle.py` | `MAX_LAUNCHES_PER_CYCLE` (35) | remove | replaced by the new-since-last-poll rule (Q1 of the design; section 4.3) |
| `cycle.py` | `DAY_CAP` (36) | keep, reshape | counts issues woken for the first time in 24 hours |
| `cycle.py` | `SCHEMA_VERSION` (54) | reshape | 2 |
| `cycle.py` | `DEFAULT_TRIGGER_LABEL`, `DEFAULT_APPROVAL_LABEL` (55-56), `_LABEL_KEYS`, `_LABEL_MAX`, `_PROJECT_ID`, `_APPROVER_LINE`, `_ACCOUNT_ID_MAX` use for approvers, `_APPROVERS_MAX_BYTES` (58-76) | remove | `_ACCOUNT_ID_MAX` stays for `owner_account_id` |
| `cycle.py` | `_TOP_KEYS` (58-68) | reshape | `schema_version, enabled, owner_account_id, orchestrator_repo, state_dir, session_tag_prefix` |
| `cycle.py` | `ConfigError` (81), `_invalid` (110), `_is_int` (114), `_is_abs_path` (118), `_decode_mount_point` (142), `fs_type_of` (146), `_read_mountinfo` (178), `_default_state_dir` (187), `check_platform` (286) | keep | none |
| `cycle.py` | `Config` (97) | reshape | drops `trigger_label`, `approval_label`, `project_id`, `approvers_file`, `worktree`; adds `orchestrator_repo`, `session_tag_prefix` |
| `cycle.py` | `_label` (127), `_approvers_invalid` (294), `validate_approvers` (298), `approvers_summary` (364) | remove | no approvers |

| `cycle.py` | `Deps` (435), `CycleResult` (449) | reshape | `launcher` becomes `waker`; `launched_issue` becomes `woken`, `would_launch` becomes `would_wake` |
| `cycle.py` | `resolve_exit` (466), `exit_for` (480), `_Skip` (491), `_Transient` (499), `_Stop` (503), `_LastResult` (527) | keep | none |
| `cycle.py` | `_Cand` (517) | reshape | drops `approval`; adds `repo` |
| `cycle.py` | `_Cycle` helpers `now`, `_ts`, `_tf`, `_tf_shared`, `_cond`, `_stopped`, `_exit`, `_set`, `_save`, `_warn` (570-608) | keep | none |
| `cycle.py` | `_decision` (621), `_issue_line` (646) | reshape | no approver id, event id or approval time in decision lines |

| `cycle.py` | `_origin_ok` (670) | reshape | takes the repo and its checkout |

| `cycle.py` | `_load` (739), `_login_step` (752), `_val` (836), `_day_cap_reached` (867), `_rv` (965), `_fail` (1018) | keep | `_val` and `_rv` lose the `scope_missing` branch |
| `cycle.py` | `_sweep` (777) | reshape | queues orphaned `pending` and unacknowledged `woken` or `unknown` entries for resend, and abandons an entry whose 3 attempts are used |

### 4.11 The hold and the acceptance check on issues

Rule (Clay's ruling 23, 2026-11-19): an issue with no plan is not ready, and a plan must exist before the issue can be claimed. The POLL handles the hold: it gives an assigned issue that lacks the three sections the label `hold:plan` and does not send it to an orchestrator while it is held. Once the hold is lifted, the issue reaches the orchestrator on the next cycle as a normal assignment. Assignment stays the only trigger (R1): the hold is a precondition on the issue, not a trigger and not an approval that anybody gives.

**1. The acceptance check.** One pure function, `plan_check(body)`, in `src/superclaude/cli/issue_watch/hold.py` (PR A). The verb `superclaude orchestrator plan-check` (5.3, the start of wave 1) imports it, so there is one definition. It reads text only: no model and no GitHub call. For each of the three headings `## Priority`, `## Fix approach` and `## Test plan` (the format of #651) it requires that the heading line occurs exactly once, that the section under it (up to the next `## ` heading or the end, with HTML comments and white space removed) is not empty, and that it is not only "Not known yet" in any letter case. It prints `ok` and `missing`: a list of the closed tokens `priority`, `fix_approach` and `test_plan`, one for each section that is absent, repeated, empty or "Not known yet". It says whether the issue is filled in. Its three failure forms are the three that plan mode (point 5, Block A-8) treats: a heading **absent** (plan mode appends it), a heading present once with an **empty or "Not known yet"** section (plan mode fills it in place) and a heading **repeated** (plan mode refuses and stops). It does not say whether the plan is good: that stays with the plan agent's brief, the worker and the reviewers.

**2. The two labels, and who changes them.** Two labels, in each listed repo; their names are constants of the code, not settings.

- `hold:plan` means "the plan is missing". The poll (step 7a of 4.5) is the only program that writes it: it adds it to an issue that fails the check and removes it when the check passes, under the grant `issue.hold` of `repos.yaml` (6.2). The poll creates the label the first time a repo needs it (read, then create when absent; a refusal is `hold_write_failed` and the issue is skipped for the cycle; U31).
