The orchestrator adds a header block that the report scripts read: `Follow-up state` (one of the FR-9 set: working, question, gated, delivered, claimed-missing, stalled, failed, not-started), `Human assignee`, `Orchestrator` (`[{{orchestrator_tag}}](orca://session/{{orchestrator_session_id}})`, or "no live session: {{reason}}" while it is down, 5.7, each followed by its tier, 5.8), `Agent session` (the worker: `[title](orca://session/{{session_id}})` or "no live session: {{reason}}", followed by its tier), `PR and CI state`, `Last update` (UTC), `Kind` (`orchestrated` or `info:<stream>`).

**`templates/escalation.md`** (wave 5 and any wave). Header: the issue link, the rule number, the one-sentence decision. Then the nine rows of the table in 5.6, each with its old rule number, so a test can fail when one is missing; each entry names exactly one row; then the three ways it reaches the human, each with a status line the orchestrator fills (`Needs you entry: written`, `Orca gate: <gate id>`, `Notice: ClickUp DM sent | DM failed, Orca unread mark set`). Body, the FR-12 shape so it can be answered alone: the question; every option with one line on what it does; the key facts with evidence; the recommendation and why; what happens after each answer; links. In the order of 5.6 the body also carries the lines `Why this reaches you:`, `Low-stakes options:` and `If you do not answer:`. The template writes these as lines of the issue's own entry in the living report, in that issue's section (ruling 24); it does not write a separate section.

**`templates/worker-checkin.md`** (wave 5). The message: "Status check for `{{repo}}#{{number}}`. Reply with `ack {{message_id}} applied` first (WC-12), then these fields." Expected fields: `State` (`working`, `question`, `blocked`, `done`), `Last step`, `Next step`, `Blocked by` (or "none"), `PR` (number or "none"), `Evidence` (a path under the report directory). The answer arrives by `orchestration check`, never by terminal text.

### 5.5 What survives a restart, and how a step is repeated safely

- Each issue has one file `.orchestrator/issues/<key>.json`: `schema`, `repo`, `number`, `orchestrator` (the id of the orchestrator that holds it, 5.7; the poll reads this field to count issues in flight), `tiers` (5.8), `status` (`in_progress`, `done`, `escalated`, `skipped`, `failed`), `wave`, `step`, `wake_id`, the ids found so far (lease comment, claim comment, plan marker, Task, Dispatch, terminal handle, worktree path, pull request number and head sha, merge commit) and an append-only `log` of `{ts, wave, step, phase, detail}` where `phase` is `intent` or `done`.
- The folder `.orchestrator/issues/` also holds plan records `plan-<key>.<requested_at>.json`, one file per plan request, never reused by a later request of the same key (`kind: plan`; status `in_progress`, `done` or `failed`; steps `plan_call_started`, `plan_written(body hash)`, `plan_done`, `plan_failed`; 4.11 point 5). The restart rule resumes every `in_progress` file of the orchestrator, state files and plan records. For a plan request the order in wave 0 is the record first (exclusive create) and the acknowledgment `taken` second, so a restart finds the record of a request whose acknowledgment was not written and resumes it; the poll's sweep (4.5 step 6) raises `plan_stuck` for a plan request that is `taken` and whose record is still `in_progress` or does not exist `PLAN_CALL_MAX_MINUTES` (60, this design's value) after the acknowledgment was first read (`plan_acked_at`). The step names of wave 2 move: wave 1 gains `plan_rechecked` (first step, before `lease_posted`) and wave 2 keeps `plan_recorded(body_sha)`.
- The state file also has the field `round` (1 at creation), the field `remaining` (the unmet criteria of the last `left_open` result, at most 20 lines of at most 300 characters) and the list `rounds` (one item for each finished round: `{round, pr, head sha, merge commit, dispatch, branch, remaining, closeout_result, audit_result}`); the step names gain `handback_recorded(round)`, `old_dispatch_released` and `handback_stopped(why)` (wave 8), `replan_started` and `replan_written(body hash)` (wave 2) and `branch_switched(name)` (wave 4); the folder `.orchestrator/` gains `handbacks/<key>.r<n>.txt` (the remaining files, mode 0600); `HANDBACK_MAX` = 3 is a constant of the skill (this design's value). The `status` set is not changed, and the restart rule of wave 0 needs no change for a handed-back issue: it is an `in_progress` file and is resumed at its recorded step. The restart rule also resumes an `escalated` state file whose last step is `closeout_failed`, once (step `closeout_retried`; a second failure leaves it `escalated` for a person).
- The file is replaced whole with a temp file and `rename`; writes hold a `flock` on `.orchestrator/state.lock`. Creation uses exclusive create, which is the second layer of DQ3.
- Rule 1: write the `intent` line before an outward act and the `done` line after it. Rule 2: on restart an `intent` with no `done` is settled by reading the outside state named in the wave (GitHub for a claim, lease, plan or merge; `worker-list` or `request-show` for a Task or worker; the file system for a worktree), never by repeating the act. Rule 3: an act whose effect is already visible outside is recorded as done and not repeated. Rule 4: a message is acknowledged in the mailbox only after it is handled and its id recorded.
- A restart (the session, Claude Code, Orca or the host) therefore does `run-use`, reads the state files, and resumes each `in_progress` issue at its step. The session may be cleared at any wave boundary; nothing needed lives only in its context. The Run id, the files and the handles are looked up again each time, because terminal handles change after an Orca restart (FR-21).
- Local test: kill the session in the middle of each wave (section 9, T5) and assert no second claim comment, no second lease winner, no second Task and no second `worker-start`.

### 5.6 Escalation and the grants

The orchestrator handles itself: sorting each worker into an FR-9 state from `worker-list`; nudging a worker by a mailbox check-in following the playbook; answering a worker's `ask` when the issue, the code and the docs answer it; merging when every condition holds; closing the issue after the merge; deleting ordinary files its work needs (the worker does, in its worktree). It reaches the human three ways, always the same three: (1) an entry in "Needs you" of the work report (the entry file of 5.4); (2) an Orca decision gate (`gate-create`), resolved only after a recorded human answer (FR-17); (3) the work report's existing notice, a ClickUp DM sent by the report skill's `notify` (the DM needs `CLICKUP_API_TOKEN`; without the token, or when the DM fails, the notice is an Orca unread mark on the orchestrator workspace with a note, `orca worktree set --worktree path:<orchestrator repo> --unread --comment <note>`, FR-12).

**What may reach Clay.** The orchestrator decides everything that is not in this table, records what it decided and why in the report entry ("Decided by the orchestrator: ..."), and goes on. When in doubt, a case does not reach Clay. A case reaches him only when it fits one row, and its entry names that one row. This table is the only list of what reaches Clay: it holds Clay's five product reasons (ruling 16, 2026-11-19), Jay's six reason classes and the eight rules of issue 655 section 9 (R13), each in its own column, so there is nothing to compare.

| Row | It reaches Clay when it needs | Covers | Was (rule of issue 655 section 9) | Clay's reason (ruling 16) | Jay's class |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | his own identity | acting or speaking as him where only he can; contact with anyone outside Ironbelly; signing in somewhere as him | rule 1 (contact outside Ironbelly) | none | identity |
| 2 | a secret | a key, token or login that only he holds or can create or rotate; any request to write a secret anywhere (NFR12) | none (new) | none | credential |
| 3 | money | spend; pricing; billing; anything that commits money | rule 1 (spend) | pricing | money |
| 4 | a legal act | a contract, terms, a filing, a signature, a compliance statement | none (new) | none | legal |
| 5 | something that cannot be undone, or that changes shared settings | publishing or tagging a release; deleting a shared branch; a force push; a repository settings change; deleting a skill or an agent that the issue does not name | rule 1 (deleting branches, force pushes, settings changes) and rule 2 | publishing or tagging a release; deleting shared branches | irreversible |
| 6 | other people's access or data | giving, removing or changing anyone's access; reading or changing their data | none (new) | other people's access and data | none (Jay's identity is his own) |
| 7 | a breaking change | a change that the issue, the pull request or a review finding calls breaking: something other people or other systems rely on (a command, a flag, a file format, a public function) stops working | none (new) | breaking changes | policy |
| 8 | a decision only he can make | a rule, priority or trade-off that changes what the product does: a scope that creeps into peripheral features beyond the issue's plan; a question still unanswered after the issue, the code, the docs and a web search; a choice between options that changes what the product does; merging over a failing check, which waits for his explicit OK; a pull request that changes a merge-safety file, which waits for his explicit OK (ruling 35, 5.3 wave 7) | rules 3, 4, 5 and 6 | none | policy |
| 9 | him because it is stuck and nobody else can unstick it (a stop, not a decision) | a worker that the check-in, the typed key and the approval did not unstick the same day; a failed handoff; a pull request held because a listed review agent never ran or finished; a merge that his rights or the repo's protection forbid | rules 7 and 8 and the cases of the old paragraph "Cases this design adds" | none | none (Jay's agent asks a peer; we have none) |

Merging and closing issues are not in the table (R10, ruling 13). Wherever this design says "escalation rule N", it means the row whose "Was" column holds N (rule 1 is rows 1, 3 and 5; rules 3 to 6 are row 8; rules 7 and 8 are row 9). The two sentences that use "rule 1" as a list of actions that are never approved or never done (wave 5 rule (6) and the 5.2 Will Not Do line) are rewritten in 5.2 and in rule (6) of wave 5; every other "escalation rule N" keeps its meaning through the table. An escalation reaches Clay in three ways, and none of them is a separate list for an orchestrated issue (ruling 24): (1) lines of the issue's own entry in the living report, in the section of that issue, which hold the escalation, its reason (the row), its options and the line `If you do not answer:`; (2) an Orca decision gate where an answer is needed; (3) the notice (a ClickUp DM, or the Orca unread mark when the DM cannot be sent). The coordinator's "Needs you" list (FR-12) still exists for what belongs to no orchestrated issue: a session of its own, a plan call that failed on an issue with no entry yet, and the cases of the daily report.

**The rules are on from the start (ruling 24).** There is no rollout ladder and no `reach_bar` key. The verb `superclaude orchestrator escalate` refuses an entry that names no row or an unknown row (exit 2, nothing written, nothing sent) and says "decide it yourself and record it". Rerunning an entry that already exists (same issue, same row, same question) is not a new ask: it is not counted.

**The daily cap.** `reach_daily_max` in `repos.yaml`: a whole number from 0 to 50, 0 meaning no cap, which is the value when the key is absent or unreadable. It limits the notices (a ClickUp DM or an unread mark) the orchestrators of one repo send in 24 hours, counted per `<short>` of 5.7 and not per orchestrator id, because ids are never reused. The count is kept in `<orchestrator-repo>/.orchestrator/asks.json`, written only by `escalate` under `.orchestrator/state.lock`, one row per notice (time, issue key, row number; no text), at most 500 rows, none dropped while it counts. A notice over the cap is held back and sent as one DM at the next hourly pass that finds a free place or at the next daily run; the entry and any gate are always written, so nothing is lost (FR-10). The cap never drops an entry. Recommended value: 5 (this design's value; Jay's own default is 0, and his digest flags a seat once it has more than five open items) (AD-B4, settled as recommended, ruling 39).

**Who may change the cap, the grants and the merge-safety list.** `reach_daily_max`, `grants` and `merge_safety_files` (5.3 wave 7) are changed only on Clay's recorded word. The `superclaude orchestrator repos` verbs refuse to write any of the three unless called with `--answer-ref <action log id>` that `answer-check` accepts (below). This is why the keys may live in a file the orchestrator itself edits: Jay keeps his out of the agent's reach, ours are inside it and are guarded by the verb and by a test.

**The shape of an entry.** FR-12's entry (question, every option with one line on what it does, key facts, recommendation and why, what happens after each answer, links) gains three lines, in this order after the question: `Why this reaches you:` (one row of the table, in words, with its number); `Low-stakes options:` (the numbers of the options that may be answered by typing a bare number, or "none"); and, after "what happens after each answer", `If you do not answer:`. For an orchestrated issue these are lines of that issue's own entry (the section for that issue in the living report). An option is low-stakes only if choosing it is reversible and touches only the orchestrator's own work; no option of rows 1 to 7 is ever low-stakes. A typed bare number answers a low-stakes option only; for any other option the orchestrator asks once more and names the option, and a bare number never counts for it. An answer is always typed (ruling 24): no tap, click or reaction answers anything.

**If he does not answer.** One rule for every row: nothing that needs Clay is decided for him (ruling 24: an unanswered question waits, no default). The item waits, nothing proceeds on that item, and the orchestrator's other issues go on. The line `If you do not answer:` says in plain words what stays blocked and what keeps going ("Issue 12 stays held and no worker starts; issues 14 and 15 continue"). It never names a default action, and the orchestrator never takes one. (This is Jay's rule in `needs-owner.md`. His separate open-questions file, as FULL-LIST.md describes it, lets the agent "keep working instead of waiting" on a stated default; that wish is met here by the other issues continuing, not by deciding for him.)

**Every answer is checked before it is acted on.** `superclaude orchestrator answer-check --entry <id> --source <session|clickup-dm> --answered-at <UTC time> [--question-key <key>] [--answer-text-file <file>]` passes only when (1) the source is Clay's: input typed in the orchestrator's own terminal by a person (FR-17's accepted v1 guard), or a ClickUp DM reply from Clay's own ClickUp user id; an answer relayed by another agent never counts; (2) it names the right entry (the entry id appears in the answer, or it is a reply in that entry's thread); and (3) it is newer than the entry's last edit and its notice. Every question that `escalate` asks carries a question key, recorded with the entry (an ordinary ask has the key `<issue>:<row>:<question>`; the merge-safety hold of 5.3 wave 7 has `merge_safety:<PR>:<head sha>`); when `--question-key` is given, the answer passes only if the key recorded for the answered question equals it exactly, so an answer to another question never stands for this one. It prints the three results (four with a question key); the action log records the source, the time and the results. `gate-resolve`, a change to `repos.yaml` keys and any step that waits for Clay run `answer-check` first. A refusal leaves the item waiting.

The standing grant (DQ6) is the `grants` list of `repos.yaml`. `superclaude orchestrator` refuses with `E-NO-GRANT` any GitHub write that the orchestrator makes through its own verbs when the action is not in the list, and each such write names the grant it used in the state file's log; the assignment and the claim comments are written by `/sc:issue` itself, which these verbs do not see. Deleting a branch, force pushing and settings changes have no grant and cannot be added by the orchestrator itself: the list is changed only on the user's word (FR-20).

### 5.7 Several orchestrators: one per repo, at most 3 issues in flight each

Owner ruling 8, 2026-11-19. There is one orchestrator per repo. Each holds at most `max_in_flight` issues (a key of `repos.yaml` per repo, default 3, 4.4). When a repo has more issues than that, the poll starts another orchestrator for the same repo. The single report session (tag `coordinator`, the daily report) is a separate thing and does not take issues. The rest of section 5 describes what one orchestrator does with the issues of its own wake; this subsection says which orchestrator that is.

**1. What "in flight" means and where it is counted.** An issue is in flight for an orchestrator from the moment the poll routes it to that orchestrator until the issue is closed, dropped or escalated to the owner. Dropped means the orchestrator skipped it after the routing (`claimed_elsewhere`, a claim stop, a repo or checkout failure); escalated means its state file has status `escalated` or `failed`; closed means `done`. The count of one orchestrator is the sum of (a) its state files `.orchestrator/issues/<key>.json` with status `in_progress` (each file has the field `orchestrator`, its id, 5.5), (b) the poll's own store entries routed to it in state `pending`, `woken` or `unknown` that have no state file yet, because the orchestrator has not read its wake (an `unknown` entry is resent like a `woken` one, 4.5 step 11, so it counts), and (c) its open plan requests: a `holds` entry of the poll's store with `plan_requested_at` set, `plan_request_orchestrator` equal to that orchestrator and `plan_request` in `pending`, `woken` or `acked` while the plan record of that request is not final, or that plan record `.orchestrator/issues/plan-<key>.<requested_at>.json` while its status is `in_progress` and its entry is not `stuck` (the record read is the one whose name is built from the `plan_requested_at` of the open request; a final record of an earlier request of the same key is never read; a plan record that cannot be parsed counts as in flight, as a state file does; a request that is `taken` and has no record yet counts, because its entry is `acked`). The count of a place for a plan request ends at the first of three things: the record of that request is `done` or `failed`; the poll raises `plan_stuck` for it (the entry becomes `stuck`); the hold ends (the `holds` entry is dropped). Nothing else can keep a place for a request. A key is counted once. The poll reads the state files read-only from `<orchestrator-repo>/.orchestrator/issues/` (files are replaced whole by temp file and `rename`, so a read never sees half a file); a file it cannot parse counts as in flight, so a damaged file can only stop new routing, never cause an extra issue. An escalated issue that returns to `in_progress` after the human answers counts again; the cap limits new routing, not the resumption of work, so an orchestrator may briefly hold more than 3 after such a return and then takes nothing new until it is back under 3.

**2. Names.** `<short>` is the repo name in lower case with every character outside `a-z0-9` removed, cut to 12 characters (`IronClaude` gives `ironclaude`); `repos.yaml` validation refuses two listed repos with the same `<short>` (`repos_invalid`). `<n>` is the smallest positive integer not yet used for that `<short>` in the registry; an id is never reused. The orchestrator id is `<short>-<n>`, the session tag is `orchestrator-<short>-<n>` (for example `orchestrator-ironclaude-1`, 25 characters; it fits `^[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,32}$`, `ccsession-tag/models.py:66`, whether that is the pattern ccsession applies to a tag is UNVERIFIED, U25). Each orchestrator has its own Orca Run, created by its wave 0 with `run-create --objective "Orchestrate <repo> issues (<id>)"` and recorded at once in `.orchestrator/runs/<id>.json` (`run_id`, `tag`, `repo`, `host`, `created_at`, `status`: `active`, `ending` or `ended`). A restarted orchestrator finds its Run this way: the poll starts `ccsession orchestrator-<short>-<n>`, the wake names `target.orchestrator`, wave 0 opens `runs/<id>.json` and runs `run-use --id <run_id>`; with no such file it is a first start and creates the Run. The registry `.orchestrator/orchestrators.json` is written only by the poll, under its lock: `{id: {repo, tag, created_at, status: active | ended}}`.

**3. Routing.** In a cycle, after the filters of 4.5 step 8 and the re-read of step 9, the poll takes `.orchestrator/state.lock` (local file work only; nothing is sent while it is held) and routes each candidate, repo by repo, in number order: it takes the repo's `active` orchestrators in id order and picks the first whose count (point 1, plus the candidates already routed to it in this cycle) is below the repo's `max_in_flight`; if there is none, because the repo has no orchestrator or every one is full, it adds a new registry entry `<short>-<n>` with the next free `<n>` and routes the candidate to it. Nothing waits in a queue: a repo with 4 issues gets orchestrator 1 with 3 and orchestrator 2 with 1. A plan request is routed after all assignments of the cycle, only to an `active` orchestrator of the repo whose count is below `max_in_flight`; when none is, nothing is sent and `plan_requested_at` is not set, and the next cycle tries again; a new orchestrator is added for a plan request only when the repo has no `active` orchestrator at all. The store entry is written `pending` with `orchestrator: <id>` and saved before anything is sent (step 10). The poll then writes one wake file for each orchestrator that has routed candidates; the wake names its target (`target.orchestrator`, `target.tag`, `target.repo`, 3.4) and holds only issues of one repo. It releases the lock and sends each wake to exactly its target: the mailbox message to `run:<that orchestrator's run id>` (none yet when `runs/<id>.json` does not exist, which for a new orchestrator is expected and not a warning), and the typed line to the terminal that carries its tag, under the idle rules of DQ2. A target that is down is started or restarted by the poll (DQ2, point 5); a target that is busy gets the mailbox message alone. When an orchestrator never acknowledges after 3 attempts the entries are `abandoned` (exit 7, unread mark) and are not re-routed to another orchestrator, because a re-route could deliver the same issue twice. New orchestrator ids are bounded by the day cap of 6 new issues in 24 hours for the whole store (`DAY_CAP` 6, `cycle.py:36`; 4.5 step 8): at most 6 new ids a day, because a new id is made whenever no active orchestrator of the repo has room and an ended id is never reused. The cap does not bound the sessions alive at one time; the limit of 3 issues each and the idle end of point 5 do.

**4. How two orchestrators never claim the same issue.** Four layers. (1) The poll is the only router and routes under one lock: a cycle holds the store lock (`flock` on `state_dir/lock`, a second cycle exits 75) and the routing holds `.orchestrator/state.lock`. An entry is written `pending` with its target before any send and is never changed to another orchestrator; a re-sent wake goes to the same one. (2) Wave 0 of an orchestrator refuses a wake whose `target.orchestrator` is not its own id (`wrong_target`, the wake is rejected and not acknowledged, 3.5), so a wrongly delivered wake takes nothing. (3) The per-issue state file is created with exclusive create (`O_EXCL`) under a file name that has no orchestrator id in it, so one host never holds two files, and two orchestrators, for one issue. (4) The claim lease of DQ3 (lowest comment id wins) stays as the second layer, and what it arbitrates is now only what the poll cannot see: two hosts that each run a poll, two users' orchestrators, a hand-run `--take`, or a store restored from a backup. It no longer arbitrates two orchestrators of one host. `--take <owner/repo#N>` typed in an orchestrator session creates the state file by (3), so it refuses an issue that already has an `in_progress` file (`already_dispatched`), and it refuses when that session already holds `max_in_flight` issues (`orchestrator_full`; open plan requests count, point 1); the poll counts the new state file like any other. For a key whose state file is `escalated` with the last step `closeout_unclear`, `closeout_failed` or `handback_stopped`, the old file is moved to `.orchestrator/issues/retaken/<key>-<UTC time>.json` (a folder the poll does not read) and then the normal exclusive create runs; an `in_progress` file (a handed-back issue included) still refuses with `already_dispatched`, and every other `escalated` file still refuses too, as today (the exclusive create finds the file): nothing is moved and no second worker can start for an issue that is waiting mid-work for a person.

**5. Lifecycle.** An orchestrator ends when it has had no issue in flight and no unacknowledged wake for 30 minutes (`IDLE_END_MINUTES`, a constant of the skill; this design's value). Its wave 9 then takes `.orchestrator/state.lock`, sets `status: ending` in `runs/<id>.json`, re-checks that no wake file addressed to it lacks its acknowledgment and no state file of it is `in_progress` (if either is true it sets `active` again and stays), sets `status: ended`, releases its dispatches (wave 9), prints the return contract and closes its own terminal with `orca terminal close --terminal <handle>` (UNVERIFIED, U26, that a session can close its own terminal; if it cannot, it stays at an idle prompt with `ended` recorded, which costs no tokens). The poll never routes to an orchestrator whose file is not `active`, and at its next cycle marks the registry entry `ended`. Because the routing and the wake files are written under the same lock, an issue cannot be routed to an orchestrator that is just ending. An orchestrator that died with issues in flight (its terminal with that tag is gone while `runs/<id>.json` is `active` and it has `in_progress` state files or unacknowledged wakes): the poll starts `ccsession orchestrator-<short>-<n> --tier <orchestrator_tier>` again with the same tag, writes a wake with no `assigned` items and `target.resume: true`, and wave 0 runs the restart rule of 5.5 over that orchestrator's `in_progress` files; the start counts as delivery attempt 1 and the 3-attempt rule of DQ2 applies. A dead orchestrator with nothing in flight is not restarted; the poll marks it `ended`. The number of live sessions therefore falls back to zero a half hour after the last issue is done.

**6. The living report.** Each entry file (wave 3) carries the human assignee, the orchestrator (id, tag and the link `orca://session/<session id>`) and the worker (the link of its session). The entry template gains the line `Orchestrator` in its header block, and the existing `Agent session` line is the worker's; the follow-up states are unchanged (the FR-9 set): an issue whose orchestrator is down is `stalled` with the reason "orchestrator <id> is down, restart in progress" until the poll has restarted it. An entry whose state file is `escalated` with the last step `closeout_unclear`, `closeout_failed` or `handback_stopped` is never `stalled` (only an issue with an `in_progress` file whose orchestrator is down is): it shows "landed, not finished" and needs no orchestrator until a person acts; every other `escalated` file (a merge held for Clay's OK, a stuck worker, a question) keeps the behaviour the spec gives it today: its entry is `stalled` when its orchestrator is down. The PR C rows of section 8 and 9 change by the two new fields (6.4).

**7. The report session.** The single `coordinator` session is unchanged: it runs `/agent-work-report`, is woken by the two existing waker automations (`coordinator-morning-wake`, `coordinator-missed-check`), reads the entry files and the registry read-only, takes no issue, routes nothing and is never counted against any orchestrator's cap.

**8. Effects elsewhere.** Wave 0 takes only the issues of its own wake. The wake message gets the field `target` (3.4) and `assigned[]` holds one repo. The poll's store (schema 2) gains `orchestrator` on each entry; `repos.yaml` validation checks `max_in_flight` (an integer from 1 to 10) and the unique `<short>`; `check-config` prints the routing it would do for the current store. The reason table gains `registry_invalid` (cycle, exit 2, 4.7) and the wake-check refusal list gains `wrong_target` (3.5). Tests (9.1): a routing test with 4 issues of one repo (3 go to orchestrator 1, the 4th starts orchestrator 2); a no-double-route test with the poll killed between routing and delivery (the next cycle resends the same wake to the same orchestrator, starts no second one and the count does not change); a dead-orchestrator test; a lifecycle test; a wrong-target test; and the `--take` tests of point 4.

**9. Effect on the pull request split.** PR A (the poll) carries the routing, the registry, the in-flight count, the start and restart of an orchestrator session, the `target` field, the `max_in_flight` and unique-`<short>` validation, the new reason and their tests: a new module `route.py` beside `wake.py` and `repos.py` (4.10). PR B (the orchestrator skill) carries the `runs/<id>.json` file, the wave 0 target check, the `--take` rules, the idle end in wave 9, the `orchestrator` field of the state file and the `Orchestrator` line of the entry and its template. PR C only renders the two new fields. The estimate in the issue body rises from 115 to 124 hours (the poll PR from 12 to 17 hours for `route.py` and about eight test cases, the orchestrator skill PR from 60 to 64 for the lifecycle and about four test cases; an estimate, not a measurement, and this design itself gives no other size figure); each stays one pull request. The local end-to-end tests of 9.2 gain one test (T10, two orchestrators for one repo).

### 5.8 Model tiers: stronger models plan, cheaper models build

Clay's ruling of 2026-11-19 03:09 UTC. The plan is written once, when the issue is created (issue 651; Clay's comment on issue 450), so execution can run on cheaper models and the better models do the planning. Until now this design chose no model for any agent it starts. The settings name ccsession tiers, never a model id: a tier is a group of models with automatic failover inside it when one runs out of usage (`ccsession --help`).

**1. The settings.** Three keys of `repos.yaml`, each at the top level as the default and again inside a repo's entry to override it: `plan_tier`, the plan call of wave 0 (default `tier1b`); `worker_tier`, the building worker of wave 4 (default `tier2`); `orchestrator_tier`, the orchestrator session the poll starts (default `tier1b`, the workspace default tier). Two optional keys, `plan_effort` and `worker_effort`, give a reasoning level for those two agents (absent means the agent's own default; the accepted levels are UNVERIFIED, U30); the orchestrator session has no effort setting. A missing key takes the default, and a repo's own value beats the top-level one.

**2. How a tier reaches each agent, from the code.** The orchestrator session: the poll types `ccsession orchestrator-<short>-<n> --tier <orchestrator_tier>`. `ccsession` reads `--tier` at `ccsession-tag/ccsession:1001-1010`, and `apply_tier_selection` (`:1407-1434`) resolves it with `models.py tier-resolve` into the session's model and window, exiting 2 for a tier the owner may not use. A restarted orchestrator is started the same way with the same tag. The plan agent and the worker: `orca orchestration worker-start --task <id> --worktree path:<existing worktree> --agent claude --model <picker id> [--effort <level>]`, where the picker id is `claude-gw-` followed by the tier name (for example `claude-gw-tier2`), the form that the issue worker agent files pin in `model:` (`sc-issue-protocol/refs/tiers.md`) and that `ccsession` exports for the aliases (`ccsession:1111-1113`). `--model` and `--effort` are options of `worker-start`; `--effort` requires `--model`, and neither combines with `--terminal` (`orca orchestration worker-start --help`, lines 26, 27 and 39). Orca's own guide says to compare `launch.requested` with `launch.effective` and never to claim a model or effort from the requested arguments (`orca skills get orchestration --reference references/coordinator-loop.md`), so the skill records what `launch.effective` shows. That an Orca-launched `claude` accepts a picker id through `--model` and that the gateway serves it is UNVERIFIED (U28); the local test of 9.2 settles it, and until it does the worker's tier is a request, not a fact. The skill builds the picker id in code from the setting, because `tests/skills/test_model_alias_policy.py` fails on the text `claude-gw-tier` in skill files outside its allow-list (`docs/reference/model-aliases.md`, line 12); if building it in code does not pass that test, the skill's folder is added to the allow-list (U29).

**3. Recording.** The state file of an issue gets a `tiers` field: for `plan` and `worker`, the tier requested, what `launch.effective` showed and, when used, `fallback_from`. The registry entry of an orchestrator in `orchestrators.json` gets its tier and `fallback_from`. The report entry's `Agent session` line ends with the worker's tier and its `Orchestrator` line with the orchestrator's (for example `tier2`, or `tier2, fallback from tier1b`).

**4. Fallback.** The tiers a user may use are the lines `ccsession --tier <name>` under "Tiers in this workspace" in `ccsession --help` (a Coder Member sees tiers 2 to 4 only, `tiers.md`). The poll reads that list when it starts an orchestrator and the orchestrator when it starts the plan agent or a worker. A configured tier that is not on the list falls once to the next listed tier after it in the order tier0, tier1a, tier1b, tier2, tier2b, tier3, tier4 (tier1b to tier2 is the fallback of `tiers.md`); the fallback is recorded as in point 3 and never fails the issue. A tier that is listed but that the gateway refuses at the start (HTTP 400 `invalid_request_error` whose message starts `ccsession tier`, `tiers.md`), or a configured tier with no listed tier after it, is a failed handoff (escalation rule 8); for the orchestrator session the poll records `no_session` and marks the workspace unread (DQ2).

**5. Validation.** `repos.yaml` validation (4.4) accepts for the three tier keys only a tier name that `ccsession --help` lists (in this workspace tier0, tier1a, tier1b, tier2, tier2b, tier3 and tier4); any other value, a model id included, is `repos_invalid`, and so is a list that cannot be read, because no session can be started without it.

**6. Pull request split.** PR A carries the validation, the reading of the tier list, the `--tier` of the orchestrator start with its fallback, and their tests; PR B carries the `--model` and `--effort` of the `worker-start` calls of wave 4 (the plan call of wave 0 has no `worker-start`: it passes `plan_tier` as the `--depth` of `/sc:issue --plan`, PR P), the `tiers` field of the state file, the entry lines and their tests.

### 5.9 The limits of the orchestrator as a control plane

The orchestrator controls Orca sessions: it lists them, reads them, sends them work, watches them and answers their prompts. This section fixes the edge of that power. The "may do" list is closed: every line names the commands that do it, and anything else needs a change to this section. The "may never" list is a requirement of the design: every line has a test that fails when it is broken (9.1). The status column says whether a line is already covered by the design, new, or in conflict with it.

**May do (closed list).**

| Line | What the orchestrator does | Commands | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| List sessions | sorts each worker it started into an FR-9 state; finds its own sessions by tag | `orca orchestration worker-list --run <id> --json`, `worker-show --dispatch <id>`; `orca terminal list --worktree path:<repo> --json`; `ccsession --list` (read only) | covered (wave 5, DQ2) |
| Read bounded transcripts | reads a worker's transcript a page at a time | `orca orchestration worker-read --dispatch <id> --source transcript --limit <n> [--cursor <c>]`; `<n>` is never more than 200 (this design's cap, taken from the 200-message page of the BotFleet page); only a Dispatch of its own Run | in part: wave 5 names `worker-read` with no limit; the limit and the own-Run rule are new |
| Send work | starts one plan agent and one worker per issue; sends a check-in; types a prompt under the playbook | `task-create`, `worker-start --task`, `send --to dispatch:<id>`, `reply`, `orca terminal send --text <prompt> --enter` | covered (waves 0, 2, 4, 5) |
| Learn whether it started, steered or queued | reads the receipt of every send and never repeats a send blind | `worker-start` outcome `ready` or `outcome_unknown` then `request-show`; the stages `input_accepted` and `turn_started` of `terminal send`, `--wait-submit <seconds>` (observes, never resends), a retry only with the reported `--retry-request` id | covered for "started" and "queued"; Orca has no known receipt for "steered" (U40) |
| Wait | blocks on its own mailbox, never on a terminal | `orca orchestration check --wait --types <list> --timeout-ms <at most 240000>` (WC-10); `orca terminal wait --for tui-idle` | covered (wave 5) |
| Interrupt a worker, in one case | stops a worker's current turn only when a major issue must be escalated to Clay: a case that fits a row of the table of 5.6, with the escalation raised in the same step (the verb writes the escalation first, then sends the interrupt; if the escalation is refused, nothing is interrupted). It never closes the terminal, stops the Dispatch or kills the session. An interrupt goes into a turn that is running, so it is exempt from the 60-second typing hold (wave 5 safety rule (2), FR-23), and only this interrupt is, only after the escalation is written and only for this case (N7) | `orca terminal send --interrupt`, run only by `superclaude orchestrator escalate --interrupt <dispatch id>` | new (ruling 25, reverses AD-C1); five places of the coordinator spec and one of this design change by an exception (C25 to C29 of 6.3) |
| Delete what its work needs | deletes ordinary files its own work needs (the worker does so in its worktree); never what N2 lists | `rm` and `git rm` inside its own runtime folders and the worker's worktree | covered (5.6 first paragraph; removal 2 was rejected by ruling 29) |
| Tag or publish a release, with a recorded OK | tags and publishes only with a recorded human OK or directive (N6) | `git tag`, `git push origin <tag>`, `gh release create`, run with the `--answer-ref` of an `answer-check` that passed for that release | new (ruling 24) |
| Answer one approval once | types the single-use yes under the seven safety rules of wave 5 | `orca terminal read --screen`, then `orca terminal send` | covered (wave 5, FR-24) |

**May never (requirement).**

| # | The orchestrator never | What that means here | Status | The test that fails if it is broken |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| N1 | remembers a permanent "always allow" | It never types or picks "always allow" or "do not ask again"; a prompt that offers only that is escalated and no key is sent. It never writes a permission rule into any `settings.json`, `settings.local.json` or file under `~/.claude`. It never adds a line to `grants` and never changes `reach_daily_max` (5.6) or `merge_safety_files` (5.3 wave 7) without a recorded answer. `grants` is a standing permission that Clay sets; it is not a grant the orchestrator remembers. | in part covered (wave 5 rule 4, 5.2 Will Not Do, 5.6); the settings files and the `repos` keys are new | the typing test of 9.1 (a prompt offering only "always allow" sends nothing and escalates); a path test: no argv of any end-to-end test writes to a path named `settings.json` or `settings.local.json` or under `~/.claude`; the `repos` verbs refuse the three keys without an accepted `--answer-ref` |
| N2 | deletes what the rulings reserve to Clay | The orchestrator may delete the ordinary files its work needs (5.6, first paragraph; ruling 25: it may do whatever it needs). It never deletes: a shared branch (`git branch -d` or `-D`, `git push --delete`, `gh pr merge --delete-branch`, and any `gh api -X DELETE` on a path under `git/refs`); another person's data (an issue, a comment, a pull request, a release or a repository, and any other `gh api -X DELETE`); a worktree or a session it did not start (`orca worktree rm`, `ccsession --rm`, `orca orchestration reset`); a skill or an agent that the issue does not name (row 5 of 5.6). It prunes its own records only through the two named steps: `run.py refresh` for `closed/` and `info/` after `info_ttl_days`, and the idle end of 5.7. A label is not data: the poll's removal of its own `hold:plan` label (4.11) is a label write under the grant `issue.hold`, and the poll's writer passes it through the same `refuse` function, which allows exactly the path `repos/<R>/issues/<N>/labels/hold:plan` for a DELETE. | covered by ruling 25 and removal 2's rejection: 5.6 keeps "deleting ordinary files its work needs" among what the orchestrator handles itself; the shared-branch, other-people's-data and skill lines are covered in prose (rule 1, wave 9, ADD5) | the argv denylist test (below) |
| N3 | changes credentials | No `gh auth login`, `logout` or `refresh`; no `coder secret` command; no `gh secret` command; no write to `~/.env`, `~/.aienv` or `~/.aienv.local`; it never prints a credential (NFR12). | in part covered (NFR12 prose); no test today | the argv denylist test; and a scan of every file an end-to-end test produced (log, wake, state, entry, PR body, comment) for the secret-shaped patterns listed in the test file |
| N4 | mints or rotates secrets | No token, key, deploy key, webhook or app installation is created or rotated: no `gh api` write to a path ending in `/actions/secrets`, `/keys`, `/hooks`, `/installations` or `/tokens`. | new | the argv denylist test |
| N5 | controls machine or VM lifecycle | No `coder` command that starts, stops, restarts, updates or deletes a workspace; no VM or host command (`shutdown`, `reboot`, `systemctl`); no `orca automations create`, `edit`, `remove` or `run` (`orca automations --help` lists list, show, create, edit, remove, run and runs; list, show and runs only read; the automations are the owner's, O3 and O4); no `orca project` verb except `setup-clone` of DQ4, and only into `clone_parent` of a listed repo. It acts on sessions of its own only: the poll starts an orchestrator; an orchestrator may release the workers it started (wave 9) and, at its idle end, close its own terminal (5.7 point 5, U26). A worker session of its own Run that ended with its work incomplete is restarted only as FR-23 says (through Orca from its Claude session id; FR-23 and the FR-10 words "an ended session is restarted only under FR-23" are not changed, ruling 29: Clay wants to be able to restart an agent that died). It never closes, stops (apart from the one interrupt of N7) or restarts a live session, and never another orchestrator's session. | in part covered (FR-10, FR-23, 5.7); automations and workspaces are new | the argv denylist test; `orca terminal close` is called only with the handle of the session's own terminal |
| N6 | tags or publishes a release without a recorded human OK or directive (added by B: row 5 of 5.6; ruling 24) | No `git tag`, `git push --tags`, `git push origin <tag>`, `gh release create`, `edit` or `delete`, no package upload (`uv publish`, `twine upload`, `npm publish`) unless the call carries the `--answer-ref` of an `answer-check` that passed for that release (a recorded OK, or a recorded directive: Clay's typed words, recorded once with the release they name). Merging a release pull request is allowed (ruling 13, DQ8). One login means that words the user typed into the issue cannot be told from the orchestrator's own text, so they are not a directive (U57). | new | the argv denylist test: each argv is refused without an accepted `--answer-ref` and passes with one |
| N7 | stops a worker, except for one interrupt | No `worker-stop`, no `worker-abandon`, no Escape or Ctrl+C sent as bytes with `terminal send --text`, and no `terminal send --interrupt` except the one `superclaude orchestrator escalate --interrupt <dispatch id>` runs: the case "a major issue must be escalated to Clay" (ruling 25, reverses AD-C1), where "major" is any case of a row of the table of 5.6 and the escalation is written first, in the same step; if `escalate` refuses the entry, no interrupt is sent. The 60-second typing hold (wave 5 safety rule (2) and the FR-23 boundary "never send into a session showing partial typing or fresh output in the last 60 seconds") does not apply to `escalate --interrupt`, because the interrupt is by definition sent into a running turn; the exemption holds ONLY after the escalation has been written and ONLY for the one case of ruling 25, and every other send, a check-in message included, stays under the hold. | in conflict with the coordinator spec in five places for that one case: FR-10's "never stops a worker" (C25, line 176), the out-of-scope list (C26, line 49), the 60-second boundary of FR-23 (C27, line 261, with this design's wave 5 rule (2)), the acceptance box of that boundary (C29, line 263) and the FR-10 proof row (C28, line 437); the rest covered by FR-10 | the argv denylist test; the `escalate --interrupt` tests (below), among them the exemption and its limits |
| N8 | writes outside `grants` or outside `repos.yaml` | As 5.2 and 5.6 already say. | covered | the existing `E-NO-GRANT` rows of 9.1 |

**The refusal, in code.** One function, `refuse(argv, answer_ref=None, caller=None)`, in `src/superclaude/cli/orchestrator/limits.py` holds the patterns of N2 to N7 as data. Every `gh`, `git` and `orca` call that a `superclaude orchestrator` verb makes passes through it (and the poll's GitHub writer passes its label removal through it) and is not run when it returns a reason (the verb exits 2 and logs the reason, never the argument values). The patterns of N6 pass only with an `answer_ref` that `answer-check` accepted; the `--interrupt` pattern of N7 passes only when `caller` is `escalate`. The same function is what the tests use over every command the fake runner saw in the local end-to-end tests (T1 to T13 of 9.2). A change to the "may never" table without a change to the patterns, or the reverse, fails the test that compares the table with the data.

**Stated limits.** The refusal sees only the commands that the verbs run. A command the model types itself with its shell is bounded by the skill's "Will Not Do", by the scripts being the one sanctioned way to do each step, and by a test that scans the skill and its refs for these commands outside the "Will Not Do" and the denylist (the denylist file is the one place they may appear). FULL-LIST.md describes Jay's landing protocol as stating its own honest limit, that it "stops a confused agent, not a determined one" (UNVERIFIED: only that description was read, not the page it summarises); the same limit applies here. Whether a hook can run `refuse` on the model's own shell calls in orchestrator sessions only is U42.

**Ruling 14 is superseded.** Ruling 14 (pickup of a dead agent's work is not needed: ccsession failover covers it) is superseded by ruling 29 of 2026-11-19 13:16 UTC, which rejects removal 5: Clay wants to be able to restart an agent that died. Nothing in this design changes the FR-23 row "Session ended, work incomplete" (restart through Orca from its Claude session id) or the FR-10 words "an ended session is restarted only under FR-23". What the interrupt and the restart need is kept in 5.9: N5 says a worker session of the Run that ended with its work incomplete is restarted only as FR-23 says, and N7 allows the one interrupt of ruling 25.

### 5.10 The closeout skill (issue 668): what wave 8 needs from it

(a) Purpose. After a pull request is merged, something must check that the work really landed, decide whether the issue is done (ruling 32 of 2026-11-19: by reading the issue's own done-criteria against what the pull request and its linked pull requests delivered, never by the words `Fixes`, `Closes` or `Refs`), close it with one note that says why when it is, and settle or report every status that can still say "running". That is the closeout skill, `/sc:closeout`. It is not designed here and is not part of 655's pull requests: it is its own IronClaude issue, **#668**, with its own spec, independent reviews and pull request, and it adopts Jay Wedgeworth's skill and script (rulings 21 and 22). A person also uses it by hand: PR #661 of issue 651 was merged by a person at 01:29:57 UTC and its `Closes #651` closed the issue at 01:29:59 UTC, but nobody settled its worker or the report entry. This section says only what the orchestrator's wave 8 needs from it. These are requirements of issue 668, not a design of the skill.

(b) The call. Wave 8 runs `Skill sc:closeout-protocol <PR>` from the repo's checkout, with `--issue`, `--dispatch` and `--entry-key` when it knows them. It passes no repo and no state file path (issue 668's spec has neither; the repo is always `origin`). Wave 8 directs the skill to `/sc:closeout` for that pull request, and the skill closes the issue when it is done (ruling 26); the orchestrator no longer runs `gh issue close`. The skill's own session writes the done verdict; the orchestrator writes none. Every worker pull request links its issue (ruling 26), with the word chosen by the worker as ruling 38 says: `Fixes <repo>#<number>` only when the pull request fully addresses the issue by the worker's own reading of the plan (in a hand-back round: of the remaining list), otherwise `Refs <repo>#<number>`; the handoff template tells the worker so (5.4). The word matters to GitHub, not to the skill: a merge into the default branch closes the issue on `Fixes`, and the skill leaves an already closed issue alone (`already_closed`, it never reopens). Matching the words `Fixes`, `Closes` or `Refs` decides nothing for the skill: it reads them as data only (ruling 32) and judges done-ness either way. The exact flags are fixed by issue 668's spec.

(c) What wave 8 relies on. These eleven rows are the contract from this side; each becomes a requirement of issue 668 and a case of T12.

| # | What wave 8 relies on |
|---|---|
| 1 | It decides whether the issue is done and closes it only then (ruling 32). A model investigates first (the issue's own done-criteria: its Fix approach, Test plan, acceptance criteria or "Done when" list; its remaining items and later stages; its comments; and the other open or merged pull requests that reference it) and lists what it checked in every verdict. A pull request that fully addresses an issue closes it (a close that already happened is not repeated, K20). One that does not leaves it open with one short marked note of what remains and hands back to the orchestrator in the result, with the unmet criteria, so the orchestrator continues the work (the skill never starts it; row 11). It escalates only a case it still cannot tell after investigating, with the one question for a person. Matching the words `Fixes`, `Closes` or `Refs` decides nothing |
| 2 | It writes one note on the issue once: the closing note that says why the issue is done, or, when the issue is not done, a short note of what remains (the issue stays open, and this is not an escalation); a rerun posts nothing |
| 3 | It reads the truth from the pull request's state, never from commit ancestry (which lies after a squash merge) |
| 4 | It works for a merge made by a person and with no orchestrator record (PR #661) |
| 5 | It settles, or reports the status of, the issue, the report entry, the Orca Task and the worker: each final, or listed as unsettled with its reason; it never stops a worker, merges or deletes anything |
| 6 | It names a person by name and never the agent (ruling 15) |
| 7 | Not merged: for a pull request that is open, closed without a merge, or not found, it writes nothing to an issue it did not land (no close, no comment) and returns a verdict that says so |
| 8 | The verdict that means "landed" is named `landed`, and every other verdict means "not landed"; the other names are issue 668's, and wave 8, the audit (5.3 wave 8, the audit step) and 5.2 rely on `landed` only. Issue 668's spec calls the verdict `MERGED`: it is this design's `landed`. Besides the verdict it returns the issue result (`issue.status` and `issue.detail`, 668's names): `closed_now` or `already_closed` (done), `left_open` (not done, with its note; not an escalation to the owner: the result carries `issue.handback: remaining` and goes back to the orchestrator, ruling 36, row 11), or `unclear` (open on purpose, escalated with the question); other values are `reopened` and `no_verdict` |
| 9 | One writer for each status: the skill writes the issue (close and comment) and the Orca Task status; wave 9 releases the worker (the skill only reads it and, for a worker still running, sends one notice and never stops it); the report entry is written only by the verb `superclaude orchestrator report-entry` (one writer, two modes, contract in 5.3 wave 8). For an issue that the skill closes, the skill calls `report-entry --key <key> --closed` for the entry, also in a hand run; that call carries only `--key` and `--closed` and no result inputs, because the result does not exist yet when it is made (issue 668's spec 12.2 row 2: the verb accepts the call without them). For an issue left open the skill does not call it (668: the entry result is `already_final`, `issue_open`). The orchestrator's own call in wave 8 comes after the closeout and the audit and always carries the results: `--closed --closeout-result <file> --audit-result <file>` for a closed issue (read-then-write: it adds the results to the entry the skill closed, and moves the entry only if the skill's call had not) and `--update --closeout-result <file> --audit-result <file>` for an issue left open (it writes the results into the open entry and does not move it); calling it twice, in either order, gives the same final state (issue 668's spec, section 3.2) |
| 10 | A rerun makes no second write of any kind and returns the same result: the done-check is not repeated once the closeout's done note is on the issue; a not-done note is posted once (its own marker); an issue that a person reopened after the done note is not closed again (`reopened`) |
| 11 | For a not-done issue (`left_open`) it hands back in its result (issue 668's spec, revision 5, ruling 36): `issue.handback: remaining` is the hand-back signal, and `issue.remaining[]` holds the unmet criteria, one line for each criterion of the issue that the pull request and its linked pull requests did not deliver, uncapped (its not-done note lists at most 10); `issue.remaining_file` is the verdict file in the skill's private temporary directory, which the skill does not delete and which is not a safe place to read later, so the orchestrator copies the criteria into its own state at once (the verb `handback` reads them from the saved result and never from that file); `issue.handback_text` is the fixed sentence for a person reading the block. The skill starts no worker, writes no plan, starts no Dispatch and counts no rounds: a rerun that finds the issue still not done returns the same criteria again and posts no second note, so the cap is the orchestrator's (5.3 wave 8, AD-D5). A `done` or an `unclear` run carries `issue.handback: none` |

(d) What the orchestrator records from its result: the verdict word (`MERGED` in issue 668's spec, this design's `landed`), `issue.status`, `issue.detail` (`closed_now`, `already_closed`, `left_open`, `unclear`, `reopened`, `no_verdict`), `issue.note` (`posted`, `already_there`, `none`), `issue.handback` (`remaining`, `none` or `null`), `issue.remaining` (the unmet criteria of a `left_open` result, one line each, row 11), `issue.handback_text` and the `escalations` list; the unsettled list stays as before. Wave 8 copies `issue.remaining` into the saved result file at once (the skill's own `issue.remaining_file` is in a temporary directory that nobody deletes and nobody keeps). Wave 8 saves the result in the state file at `closeout_done`, so a restart reuses it and does not call the skill again, and passes the saved result file to `report-entry` (`--closed` or `--update`, by the issue result) as `--closeout-result`. The field names are issue 668's to fix.

(e) The dependency. Issue 668's pull request must be merged before the live test (PR E). Until then PR B's tests use a fake closeout.

(f) The fallback (K17, U48): the orchestrator session itself calls the skill (`Skill sc:closeout-protocol`). The orchestrator is a standing-grant coordinator and not a worker (ADD1), and the skill's writes, closing the issue and the one comment, fall under its grants `issue.close` and `issue.comment` (DQ6, 6.2). `/sc:closeout` is on the never-as-worker list (issue 668's spec, decision D12, and a pinned test there): a worker can neither call it nor be asked to. So, when the skill cannot be called from inside a wave (U2, K17) or the call fails, wave 8 runs none of the skill's steps itself and never hands the closeout to a worker: it makes the second attempt in the same turn and, if that fails too, records the step `closeout_failed`, sets the issue's state file to `escalated` (so the issue leaves the places in flight, 5.7) and escalates as row 9 of 5.6 at once with the line "run `/sc:closeout <PR>` by hand"; wave 9 still releases the worker, a restart reads `closeout_failed` and tries once more, and a person runs the skill. Calling the closeout's scripts directly with no verdict gives `escalated` with `why=no_verdict` and writes nothing (issue 668's spec), so the orchestrator never runs the scripts itself instead of the skill.

### 5.11 The audit after a pull request lands

The merge gate (wave 6) runs before the merge. The audit runs after it, on the pull request that landed, and looks at what happened on the way. It is a verb of the `superclaude orchestrator` group (ADD6): `superclaude orchestrator audit --repo <R> --pr <N> [--json]`. It is computed from GitHub with no model: one GraphQL read (a query document; the verb refuses any document that is not a query) of the pull request's commits, review threads with their comments and who resolved them, review requests, latest reviews, force-push and review-dismissed events, and comments; and one REST read (`GET repos/<R>/commits/<merge sha>`) of the files the merge commit changed. The decision is one pure function, `check_pr(pr, files)`, that takes those two replies and returns `violations` and `warnings`; it is tested on canned replies. It writes nothing to GitHub. It audits the pull request of an orchestrated issue, whoever merged it, and any pull request a person names by hand; it is not run over all pull requests (the sweep is not adopted).

**The checks.** "Human" below means an account whose GraphQL type is `User`; the review agents (`augmentcode[bot]`, `chatgpt-codex-connector[bot]`) are Apps.

| Check | What it finds | Grade | Already held by the merge gate? | Taken from |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| AU1 force push | a head-ref force-push event on the pull request, with who, when and the two shas | violation | partly: `head_moved` catches a push after the last gate read, never one before it | V1 |
| AU2 review dismissed | any review dismissal, with who dismissed whose review and its state | warning (our rules never dismiss; the user's hand and the automation share one login, so a dismissal cannot be told from Clay's own) | no | V2, adapted |
| AU3 thread resolved with no real reply | a resolved thread with no comment from anyone but the first author and no commit after the thread opened (errs toward not flagging, as Jay's does) | violation | no: the gate checks that no thread is open, not how one was closed | V3 |
| AU4a merged over a change request | a human `CHANGES_REQUESTED` review is the latest state when the pull request merged | violation | yes, from now: the gate reason `changes_requested` is a hard refusal (ruling 27, below); the audit still finds a merge that the gate did not make | V4 |
| AU4b merged with a pending review request | a pending review request to a human | warning | no | V4, graded lower |
| AU4c merged with an unresolved thread | one or more threads still unresolved at merge | violation | yes (`threads_open`); the audit confirms it, and it fires only for a merge the gate did not make | V4 |
| AU5 conflict markers | lines the merge commit added that are conflict markers (`<<<<<<< `, `>>>>>>> `, a bare `=======`) | violation | no | V5 |
| AU6 baseline or test loosened | a file whose name holds `baseline` or ends `.snap` was added or changed, or a file under a `tests/` or `test/` folder, or named `test_*.py`, `*_test.py`, `*.test.*` or `*.spec.*`, was deleted | warning | no | V6 |
| AU7 merge-safety control changed | a file of the repo's merge-safety list changed, matching both `filename` and `previous_filename` of each file of the merge commit (a rename out of a protected path counts), and a file list that is missing, failed or short is `unreadable` and never clean (the `merge_safety_files` field of `repos.yaml`, 5.3 wave 7; the recommended default is CI workflows, branch protection and ruleset files, CODEOWNERS and the files the repo's own merge gate reads, which for IronClaude are `.claude/settings.json` and anything under `src/superclaude/hooks/` or `plugins/superclaude/hooks/`, because 7.3 requires an independent review of any change to the guard) | warning for a merge that the hold of wave 7 did not stop, such as a person's merge ("needs Clay's eyes"); the orchestrator's own merge of such a pull request waits for Clay's OK first (ruling 35, 5.3 wave 7) | yes, from now: wave 7 holds a pull request that changes a file of the repo's merge-safety list; the audit finds the merges that the hold did not cover | V7, extended |
| AU8 a review app was asked to change the branch, and a commit by it followed | a comment by a human with `@codex` followed by anything but `review` (the request), and a commit on the pull request made after that comment whose author or committer is the review app `chatgpt-codex-connector[bot]` (the bot-fix commit); both come from the comments and commits that the audit's one GraphQL read already covers, with the commit's author and committer fields added to the query if it does not return them (U58) | warning (AD-E2 settled by ruling 34: yes, flag it as a warning; it never blocks a merge). The line in the entry says, in plain words: "A review app was asked to change this branch (<link to the comment>) and a commit by it followed (<sha>); check that the change was wanted." | no | V8, Codex line only |

Not applied: Jay's allowed-dismissers file and seat-App registry; every Kody line; Augment's own fix words (unknown, U53). An unreadable reply, a failed call, or a file whose `patch` is missing (a very large diff) is never read as clean: the verb prints `unreadable` for that check, the contract says `audit: incomplete`, and the pull request is not marked clean.

**Where the result goes.**

1. Always into the entry: `report-entry` writes, `--closed` for a closed issue and `--update` for an issue left open (5.3 wave 8), one line into "Done, with evidence" (and the same into "How I know") from the verb's JSON: "Audit after landing: clean, 10 checks" or "Audit after landing: 2 findings: AU3 thread on <path> resolved with no reply; AU6 test file <path> deleted", each with a link, or "Audit incomplete: <reason>". No new slot is added to the entry file, so PR C's `render.py` and `check.py` need no change for it.
2. An escalation (row 8 of 5.6, a decision only he can make: revert, or accept) only for a violation that leaves the merged result possibly broken or unreviewed: AU4a, AU4c and AU5 (AD-E1, decided by ruling 27). It is written as lines of the issue's own entry, in its section of the living report, not in a separate list (ruling 24). One escalation per pull request, never one per finding. AU1 and AU3 are violations that stay in the closed entry. Warnings never reach him (AD-E1).
3. No follow-up issue is opened by the orchestrator: there is no grant for creating issues in `grants` and the follow-up of #665 was a person's act. The entry says "a follow-up issue can be opened on your word".
4. The verb's JSON is kept at `<orchestrator-repo>/.orchestrator/audits/<key>.json` (mode 0600) for the record; for a hand-back round n above 1 (5.3 wave 8, ruling 36) the file is `<key>.r<n>.json`, so no round's audit overwrites an earlier one.
Exit codes: 0 clean or warnings only; 1 at least one violation; 2 the audit could not be completed.

### 5.12 The naming rule (owner ruling 15, 2026-11-19)

Nothing that the poll, the orchestrator or a worker it started writes where a person reads it names the agent or the model that did the work. "Where a person reads it" means: a GitHub comment, a pull request body, a commit message, a report entry and its page, a "Needs you" entry, a ClickUp notice. Not allowed: a model identifier (`claude-gw-tier2`, any `claude-...`, `gpt-...`, `gemini-...`), the product name of an agent or model family, a "generated with" or co-author line for an agent (the one exception is the `Co-Authored-By: Claude Code <noreply@anthropic.com>` trailer that this repository's practice puts on commits, ruling 39). People are credited by name: the author of the pull request, whoever merged, whoever answered. The names of the review apps (`Augment`, `Codex`, `augmentcode[bot]`, `chatgpt-codex-connector[bot]`) are facts about the reviewers and are allowed. A tier name is allowed in one place only, the `Agent session` and `Orchestrator` lines of the entry (5.8); the model string that `launch.effective` shows is never shown to a person (AD-X2).

Where the existing design writes text that a person reads, and what the rule does to each:

| Place in the design | Could it name the agent? | What the rule changes |
|---|---|---|
| The two claim comments of `/sc:issue` (wave 1: `claim-comment.md`, `accuracy-check-comment.md`) | Not in the template read for this design (`claim-comment.md` of issue 651 holds none); `next-steps.md` starts with a `/model` line that names a tier, but it is a file in the claim folder, linked by path and not posted; the model-written accuracy text is not checked | the scan of the writer below applies to the posted text; the claim templates are unchanged |
| The lease comment (DQ3) | no: a marker with the host and Run id | none |
| The closeout comment (D) | it is written by the closeout skill, not by the orchestrator | none here: the skill's own scan and test belong to issue 668 (requirement 6 of 5.10: a person by name, never the agent) |
| The handoff prompt to the worker, and through it the worker's pull request body and commit messages | yes: the session's own tools may suggest a "generated with" or co-author line | item 5 of the nine sections of `handoff-prompt.md` gains: "Never add an attribution line, trailer or badge that names an agent or a model to a commit message, a pull request body or a comment; leave out any such line a tool suggests" (with the one exception of the repository's commit trailer, ruling 39) |
| Report entries (`report-entry-update.md`): the `Agent session` and `Orchestrator` lines, "How I know", every model-written part | yes: the session line carries the tier (5.8); a model-written part might quote a model name | the tier name only, never `launch.effective`'s string; the scan of the writer below |
| Escalation text (`escalation.md`) and the ClickUp notice | yes: it quotes a worker's words, which may say what it is | the scan of `escalate`; quoted data in a fence headed "Quoted from the issue" or "Quoted from the worker" is skipped by the scan |
| The report page and the poll's `status` output | only through the entries | none |
| The wake file, the typed line, the mailbox messages, the state files, the audit log | internal, not read by a person as a report; `launch.effective` is recorded in the state file (5.8 point 3) | none; these are not "where a person reads it" |

**The test.** One function, `names_check(text)`, in `src/superclaude/cli/orchestrator/names.py`, holds the deny list (model-identifier patterns and agent product names, as data) and the allow list (the review apps). It skips text that is inside a backtick span, a link title or a fence headed as quoted data, because an issue whose title holds a product name is a fact about the repo and not a signature. It is used three ways: (1) the orchestrator's writer verbs `report-entry` and `escalate` run it on the text they are about to write and refuse (exit 2) a text that fails; (2) a test runs it over every template and fixture of the skill and over every file and every `gh` and `orca` body that the end-to-end tests produced; (3) a test feeds a fake `launch.effective` that holds a model identifier and asserts that it appears in no entry, comment, pull request body or page. The deny list is data and its false-positive rate is measured on the last 50 issue titles of the three listed repos (U54). A worker's own pull request body cannot be scanned before it is written; the handoff line above is its rule (AD-X1, decided by ruling 28: no ninth audit check unless it happens).

## 6. Changes to the coordinator spec, and the orchestrator repo

### 6.1 Rule

The coordinator spec `SPEC-AGENT-WORK-COORDINATOR-20261027.md` moves to `<orchestrator-repo>/.dev/specs/` (same relative path, so its own references stay valid) and is changed as 6.3 says. The IronFlow copy at `.dev/specs/SPEC-AGENT-WORK-COORDINATOR-20261027.md` is then updated with the same text and is not the home; a check, `diff -q` of the two files, runs in the IronFlow change. Line numbers below are those of the IronFlow copy read for this design (530 lines). "Old" is the exact text; "New" replaces it. Where the spec says "Clay" the text stays.

### 6.2 The orchestrator repo: contents and who keeps them

| Path | Holds | Kept by |
|---|---|---|
| `repos.yaml` | `orgs`, the repo list, the repo-to-checkout map with merge settings and `max_in_flight`, `grants`, `info_ttl_days` (4.4) | the orchestrator, through `superclaude orchestrator repos`, on the user's word (FR-20) |
| `overviews/<owner>__<repo>.md` | a short overview of what the repo is and does, changed when a repo is added, removed or updated | the orchestrator |
| `.dev/specs/SPEC-AGENT-WORK-COORDINATOR-20261027.md` | the coordinator spec, moved from IronFlow and changed per 6.3 | the person, by pull request |
| `.dev/coordinator/` | the work report state moved from IronFlow: `runs/`, `action-log.jsonl`, `priorities.json`, the ownership map, `report.html`; new: `orchestrated/` and `orchestrated/info/` and `orchestrated/closed/` (DQ12, DQ13); the daily run's records | the report skill (`agent-work-report`) and the orchestrator |
| `.orchestrator/` (git-ignored) | `runs/<orchestrator id>.json` (one per orchestrator, 5.7), `orchestrators.json` (the registry, 5.7), `issues/`, `wakes/` and `wakes/done/`, `handoffs/`, `state.lock` (3.1, 5.5), `asks.json` (the count of notices, 5.6; written only by `escalate`), `audits/<key>.json` (the result of the audit, 5.11; kept by the orchestrator), `handbacks/<key>.r<n>.txt` (5.5) | the poll script (wakes, registry) and the orchestrators |
| `CLAUDE.md`, `README.md`, `.gitignore` | the repo's purpose, the session rules (no secret in any file, never stage `.orchestrator/`), what is tracked | the person |

Created outside IronClaude, by the person with the rights to create a repository (creating a repo is not an agent action); the owner-approved repo is the private `IronbellyOrg/IronOrchestrator`, which already holds `repos.yaml`. Minimum for first use: `repos.yaml` with `schema_version`, `orgs`, an empty or filled `repos` list, `grants`; `.gitignore` with `.orchestrator/`.

Fields to add to the created `repos.yaml`. The file at `/home/coder/workspaces/IronOrchestrator/repos.yaml` (read 2026-11-19) has `schema_version`, `orgs`, a `users` block holding `bots: []` and, for each of its three repos, `repo`, `enabled`, `checkout`, `default_branch` and `overview`. It lacks the keys below. Global keys first, then the per-repo keys with the value for each repo; a value marked UNVERIFIED is a reading of evidence that does not settle it, and the orchestrator confirms it with `superclaude orchestrator repos refresh` before relying on it. Keys that are not listed (`working_hours`) are not part of this design. Lines to remove from the file as it is (owner ruling 11, 2026-11-19: only the user's own login counts as the user): the line `users:`, the comment line `# Bot logins that count as the user's own agents. Empty until the owner names them.` and the line `  bots: []`; the comment line above them about the login of `gh api user` may stay.

| Global key | Type | Value to add | Read by |
|---|---|---|---|
| `grants` | list of strings | `[issue.assign, issue.comment, issue.body.append_plan_sections, issue.hold, pr.merge, issue.close]` (DQ6, 5.6). The name `issue.body.append_plan_sections` is kept as a constant of the spec, but the grant now covers three body writes: appending an absent section, filling an existing empty or "Not known yet" section in place (4.11 point 5), and the re-plan of a hand-back, which replaces the text of `## Fix approach` and `## Test plan` of a claimed issue with the remaining scope (PR P, `--remaining`; ruling 36); it never covers a rewrite of any other part of the body | orchestrator (`E-NO-GRANT`) |
| `info_ttl_days` | integer | `14` (DQ13) | orchestrator (information entries) |
| `plan_tier` | tier name | `tier1b` (Clay, 2026-11-19 03:09 UTC; 5.8) | orchestrator (wave 0, the plan call) |
| `worker_tier` | tier name | `tier2` | orchestrator (wave 4) |
| `orchestrator_tier` | tier name | `tier1b` | poll (orchestrator start) |
| `reach_daily_max` | integer 0 to 50 | `5` (AD-B4, settled as recommended); 0 means no cap; changed only on Clay's recorded word | `superclaude orchestrator escalate` |

| Per-repo key | Type | IronClaude | IronFlow | IronOrca | Read by |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `clone_parent` | absolute path | `/home/coder/workspaces` | `/home/coder/workspaces` | `/home/coder/workspaces` | orchestrator (DQ4); the parent of each existing `checkout` |
| `merge_method` | `squash`, `merge`, `rebase` | `squash` | `merge` (UNVERIFIED: 2 merged PRs, both merge commits) | `merge` (UNVERIFIED: 3 merged PRs, all merge commits) | orchestrator (wave 7) |
| `release_head` | branch name or null | `integration` | `null` | `null` (PRs go to the branch `aidev02`; no release branch seen) | orchestrator (wave 7) |
| `release_merge_method` | `squash`, `merge`, `rebase` or null | `merge` (owner ruling 5; #658 was squashed and reverted by #662) | `null` | `null` | orchestrator (wave 7) |
| `protected` | boolean | `false` (`branches/master`) | `false` (`branches/main`) | `false` (`branches/main`; `aidev02` also `false`) | orchestrator (record only) |
| `can_merge` | boolean | `true` (`maintain`) | `true` (`admin`) | `true` (`admin`) | orchestrator (wave 7) |
| `reviewers` | `none` or list of `augment`, `codex` | `[augment, codex]` (both reviewed #641) | `[augment]` (UNVERIFIED: Augment on #1 and #3) | `[codex]` (UNVERIFIED: Codex on #11, no review on #8) | orchestrator (wave 6) |
| `merge_safety_files` | list of glob patterns | `.github/workflows/**`, `.github/rulesets/**`, `.github/settings.yml`, `CODEOWNERS`, `.github/CODEOWNERS`, `docs/CODEOWNERS`, `.claude/settings.json`, `src/superclaude/hooks/**`, `plugins/superclaude/hooks/**` (AD-E5; the default when the key is absent) | the default list, plus the files the repo's own merge gate reads, which the repo lists when `repos.yaml` is filled in (UNVERIFIED which) | the same as IronFlow | orchestrator (waves 6 and 7); changed, or emptied, only on Clay's recorded word |
| `max_in_flight` | integer 1 to 10 | `3` (ruling 8) | `3` | `3` | poll (5.7) |

Every other org repo is UNVERIFIED for all of these keys and stays out of the file until the owner names it (an unlisted repo is skipped, `repo_not_listed`).

The repo's name is the owner's choice (OD8, decided: `IronbellyOrg/IronOrchestrator`). Nothing in IronClaude names it: the poll's configuration file holds its absolute path on each host.

### 6.3 Exact changes

| # | Where | Old text | New text |
|---|---|---|---|
| C1 | Section 2 overview, line 54 | `The coordinator is three Claude Code skills in IronFlow (Q1) with separate jobs (CLAY)` | `The coordinator is three Claude Code skills in IronClaude (Q1, issue 655) with separate jobs (CLAY)` |
